Some are calling this 'Tuchel 2.0' and his latest squad does feel like that. Here's why England think a tilt at the European Championship is within reach, writes IAN LADYMAN

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published: 17:35 EDT, 6 November 2026 | Updated: 17:39 EDT, 6 November 2026

One of Thomas Tuchel’s favourite analogies when it comes to the development of his England team is that of a train leaving a station with only the chosen few on it. As time moves on, the bodies lying on the tracks are growing greater in number.

Consider this statistic. Of the 26 players who travelled to the World Cup in America 13 are not in the squad chosen by England manager Tuchel for next week’s Nations League games against Croatia and Spain. And that does not include Cole Palmer and Phil Foden who, after missing out in the summer, were expected by many of us to have forced a way back in by now.

Tuchel’s latest selection has been affected by injuries, of course. But more significant is that the experienced German believes he is now starting to see an England team and squad develop in a way and in a direction that even he hadn’t envisaged once Argentina ended his World Cup hopes in that dismal Atlanta semi-final in July.

Different feelings, different style of play and, to a degree, different players.

Some are calling this ‘Tuchel 2.0’ and it does feel a little bit like that. The start of the second phase of Tuchel’s tenure, the start of the road towards a tilt at another trophy, the European Championships of 2028.

Having admitted to some worry ahead of the four-game international break FIFA saddled us all with last month, Tuchel sat down yesterday looking and sounding like a man once again certain of himself.

Tuchel sat down with the media looking and sounding like a man once again certain of himself

The inclusion of Jobe Bellingham — Jude’s younger brother (right) — was the headline act but there were other messages written within

Certain enough of a direct, quick, athletic style of play that helped England to two wins over Czech Republic and one – by seven goals – against Croatia in Rijeka. And certain that a dependence on players like Jordan Henderson, Dan Burn and John Stones for what we may politely call ‘squad culture management’ lies now in the past.

All of this may crash down in Madrid next Sunday. Spain have already beaten Tuchel’s England once – at Wembley in late September – and a repeat this month would ask familiar questions about England against the really good teams.

But for now, Tuchel is confident enough to claim that he has found his path.

‘We said at some point we need to take the next step, to build on what we have, to get better and to improve otherwise you’re just left behind,’ said Tuchel yesterday.

‘That’s how football works. You have been champion in one season but you need to improve, you need to bring in new players for competition, young players, new energy, fresh energy. It’s also the way, naturally, the national team goes.

‘We cannot be stuck in the same personnel and in the same style of play.

‘I agreed with you last month. I was the first to admit I was not 100 per cent happy with the pure level of football we played in the US. I saw a higher level in training [than on the field], I saw a higher level in glimpses.

‘We adjusted some things tactically, created some new positions and took the next steps.’

The Tuchel we sat down with in late September still wore the scars of the summer’s disappointment – and the criticism that came with it – rather visibly. It was the first time we had seen him like that in his two years in the job. He was tetchy and easily provoked. Call it the Argentina effect if you like.

Here, he appeared rather healed. Good results tend to do that for a manager and a little time helps too. If he believes his team has moved on then maybe he has also and he had to.

Certainly, his selection squad speaks to that. The inclusion of Jobe Bellingham – Jude’s younger brother – was the headline act but there were other messages written within.

Henderson and Burn are available but not included. So too Arsenal’s Noni Madueke, excluded to the benefit of the more direct Liverpool winger Rio Ngumoha.

‘We need runners who will go between the lines,’ said Tuchel. A clear indication, there, that too much of England’s summer wing play was too slow and too conservatively narrow.

Palmer, Foden and Kobbie Mainoo are also on the outside looking in once again and the case of the Manchester United midfielder is perhaps indicative of how Tuchel now operates.

Moved to apologise to him in front of the whole squad in July for his lack of minutes at the World Cup, Tuchel was determined Mainoo would play in the opening four games of the Nations League campaign.

‘I was desperate to give him the minutes and desperate to include him in this new start,’ explained Tuchel.

‘But then he pulled out. He missed an excellent camp and also entered a difficult, difficult spell for himself.

‘Man United fell off a cliff a little bit performance-wise. So this is why he now suffers.’

We can talk about the whys and wherefores of that logic. If Mainoo was good enough in September then why is he not good enough now? How desperate could Tuchel really have been to play him that one withdrawal with injury closed the door for the next camp and opened it instead for Bellingham jnr?

This, though, is how Tuchel works. He is an adept tactician but he’s also a mood coach, someone who – after sobering club experiences at places like PSG, Chelsea and Bayern Munich – places huge emphasis on environments and gut feels and culture. If he feels something works, he doesn’t like to pick it apart without good reason.

This is why for example the growing voice and influence of Elliot Anderson has left Jordan Henderson suddenly surplus. Tuchel says the door is not closed on a player who will be 38 by the time the next Euros are played and he called him this week to say so. In Tuchel’s mind, though, England will now move without the veteran and others of his ilk.

‘I was concerned before the last camp that we missed the players who kept our standards,’ said Tuchel.

‘I was worried that we missed so many of them. But then this group found their own way and their own voices.

It’s also reasonable to ask why our head coach can’t find room in his squad – if not his team – for the game changing duo of Palmer and Foden

‘I know what the others did. They built a platform on which these ones can build.’

Time will tell. To a degree, Spain will tell. Can England play the kind of higher tempo, front foot football we saw against Croatia and the Czechs against the world and European champions next Sunday? They embarrassed us when we didn’t have the ball at Wembley.

It’s also reasonable to ask why our head coach can’t find room in his squad – if not his team – for the game-changing duo of Palmer and Foden. England lacked good substitutes at the World Cup. There was too much like for like.

England need to win in order for such decisions to be vindicated and Tuchel knows that.

Asked yesterday if he felt as invigorated as he looked he said he did. ‘The last camp gave me a lot of energy, gave me a lot of extra belief,’ he said.

‘It gave me a lot of optimism to not compromise on the way England teams should play. Be physical but also step up in the pure level of performances.’

It’s certainly time for this now. The summer only served to expose English shortcomings. Tuchel’s train must arrive in Madrid next weekend, not only with fresh personnel but also new ideas clearly apparent.

concrete pairs with brushed metal for hybrid automotive gallery café in hong kong

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

studio ruei designs macchina gallery café showcasing rotating automobiles against raw industrial interiors.

Nina Milhaud edited by christina vergopoulou November 6, 2026 3 min

Designed by Yuisze Wong of Studio Ruei, Macchina is a hybrid gallery café in Hong Kong’s Wan Chai district that integrates automotive exhibition spaces with hospitality programming. Framing vehicles as cultural and industrial artifacts rather than commercial commodities, the interior provides a social venue where automotive design, mechanical engineering, and specialty coffee converge.

Behind a restrained street-facing envelope, expansive glazed apertures create direct sightlines into the main display bay, framing a single vehicle as the focal sculpture, inaugurated by the Aston Martin Valkyrie. Studio Ruei organizes the internal layout and circulation through calibrated visual corridors, connecting the central vehicular plinth to surrounding lounge seating, curated display cases, and the primary espresso bar. Rotating vehicular displays introduce distinct engineering narratives over time without requiring physical alterations to the surrounding architecture.

Drawing from Brutalist precedents, the spatial enclosure utilizes a tactile palette of textured concrete, brushed metal, and natural timber, pairing industrial finishes with precise architectural reveals that reflect automotive manufacturing tolerances. Overhead, an integrated ceiling grid pairs diffused wash lighting with directional spotlighting to delineate vehicle surfaces and contours under studio-grade illumination. Recessed acoustic panels across the walls and ceiling temper reverberation, balancing acoustic comfort across the café with the focused atmosphere of an exhibition gallery.

The Cybertruck’s Death Is Actually Great News for EVs

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Tesla will end production of the Cybertruck at its Texas Gigafactory next year.

It’s a uniquely toxic electric vehicle, between its divisive design and Elon Musk’s embrace of you name it. That toxicity certainly contributed to subpar sales, along with a number of recalls.

But the end of the Cybertruck is also another sign that the era of big EVs—particularly big trucks—is waning. That’s great news for pedestrian and biker safety, but also for the battery supply chain at a time when more people need to get behind the electric wheel.

The Cybertruck uses a massive 123-kilowatt-hour battery that clocks in at around 1,600 pounds, or around a quarter of the vehicle's weight. That’s on par with the battery that powered Ford’s F-150 Lightning, another electric truck that was canceled late last year. (The company also killed a three-row EV in 2024.) Ford’s behemoth batteries ranged from 98 kilowatt-hours up to 131 kilowatt-hours.

Seeing both trucks canceled within a year of each other, while automakers have announced a bevy of smaller EVs and gas-powered autos with electric motors that can extend range, indicates there may be a shift afoot. Ford is planning to launch the Fathom next year. The sub-$30,000 electric pickup is expected to be about the size of the automaker’s compact gas-powered Maverick pickup. Though Ford hasn’t announced the battery sizes available in various trims, it’s a safe bet they won’t be anywhere near the size of the F-150 Lightning's or Cybertruck's. The company is also planning to relaunch the Lightning as an extended-range model.

Startup Slate is also launching a tiny electric pickup that can be converted to an SUV, with a bare-bones base model starting below $25,000. It also features a svelte battery and has seen a massive surge in preorders—though, to be fair, so did the Cybertruck, and we saw how that turned out.

Then there’s the Rivian R2, which launched this year. While the SUV isn’t as tiny or cheap as Ford and Slate’s vehicles—its battery has 88 kilowatt-hours of capacity and weighs in at around 1,200 pounds—it’s still smaller than the company’s flagship R1S. Rivian has also teased the R3, an even more-slimmed down SUV, in the coming years.

That’s not to say big EVs are dead. The Kia EV9, for example, is among the top 10 EVs by sales in the US, as is the R1S. There’s also a torrid resale market for the F-150 Lightning. But the rest of the top 10 new EV sales are all crossovers, sedans, or mid-sized SUVs with moderately sized batteries, and a number of newly announced EVs are trending on the smaller side or function as extended-range vehicles.

Building smaller vehicles is, of course, one way to make EVs cheaper. With less weight to propel, you can rely on a smaller battery. And given that the battery pack is the most costly component of an EV, that can in turn help automakers offer more affordable vehicles at a time when the price of so many other consumer goods is skyrocketing. That includes the current crop of EVs: The average transaction price for a new model is now $55,800, according to Cox Automotive.

Going small—or smaller, anyway—also means making the most of a limited battery supply chain. The vast majority of Cybertruck drivers are doing the same thing with their vehicles as Hyundai Ioniq 5 drivers: getting groceries, commuting, and driving kids to soccer games. But a standard-range Ioniq 5 driver is doing it with a battery about half the size.

After President Donald Trump’s administration killed EV incentives last year, sales plummeted. The Iran War and rising gas prices have led to an uptick in interest, though sales still lag behind the global average. (Used hybrid sales have also picked up, reflecting the pain people have been feeling at the pump.) Even with a Democratic-controlled Congress, EV incentives coming back under Trump are about as likely as defense secretary Pete Hegseth putting pronouns in his email signature. But should Democrats take the White House in 2028, marking the culmination of Woke 2.0, support for would-be EV buyers becomes much more likely.

Automakers pivoting to smaller cars could be much better positioned to meet pent-up demand if that happens, while also lowering the risks of delivery delays.

Even Elon’s announcement killing the Cybertruck points to where the market is heading: He claims Tesla is ending production to make room for cranking out more Cybercabs. The battery size in those vehicles? Just 48 kilowatt-hours.

Kasper Hogh riddle continues to fox Celtic as Martin O'Neill opens up on misfiring striker

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

“Kasper had a brilliant game. That's the life of a striker - you have to be there to take them. His all-round game today was very good. He gave us a platform to play.”

Sunday, August 9, the words of Celtic assistant coach Shaun Maloney after watching striker Kasper Hogh score a 33-minute hat-trick in a 5-1 win over Kilmarnock.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Hogh had not long arrived at Celtic from Bodo/Glimt for £12 million, a record signing for the Scottish champions. His predatory instincts in Ayrshire led many to believe the Dane was on his way to the golden boot.

Fifteen games on for Celtic, Hogh has not scored a goal since. In fact, his manager Martin O’Neill is yet to see him find the net, as he was recovering from a “small procedure” for the match at Rugby Park.

Maybe the visit of Kilmarnock, who he terrorised on that summer’s afternoon, on Sunday will be the match where Hogh finally finds the net again. That is, of course, if the 25-year-old gets the nod in attack.

Hogh’s star is waning. The 25-year-old has been eclipsed by fellow July signing Camilo Duran, who has netted 13 times this season and clambered off the bench to help Celtic defeat Besiktas on Thursday night. The Colombian did more in his half-hour cameo than Hogh did in double that time. The poor hitman didn’t touch the ball once in the Turkish penalty box in the opening period.

It would be unfair to judge Hogh wholly on his goals, although at £4m per strike so far, that is an expensive outlay. Physical and hard-working, he offers Celtic a focal point. O’Neill was pleased with his performance against Besiktas. “I want centre-forwards to be able to hold it and that is what he did,” said the manager. “He is not Duran, he is not a dribbler. But if he can do this for us against strong opposition it will stand him in good stead.”

Hogh’s belief is bound to be low. How could it not be? He is out of the Denmark squad for the first time in over a year and when he tapped in from an already rescinded free-kick against Besiktas, there was a slightly sarcastic cheer from the home crowd. The misfiring forward is a real talking point.

“I think Thursday night will be good for him,” continued O’Neill. “I said to him that I’m not worried about whether he scores a goal. Of course you want centre-forwards to score and of course centre-forwards live on them. But I said to him that last night I wanted him to be strong and fight when the ball is played up to him. I thought in terms of holding the ball up and bringing people into the game that he did really well.

“Centre-forwards need a goal. But that performance there against European opposition will give him confidence. I don’t know what side we will play him on Sunday. But this lad has scored goals in the Champions League, remember. He will get a confidence boost from last night in terms of doing that part of the game well. But it will be nice for him to score a goal.”

The problem Hogh perhaps had is that O’Neill will turn to Duran more often than not now. “At this minute, he is on fire,” added O’Neill of the South American. “He can play wide right, he has done that. He has played wide left. But centre forward is where he is dynamic.” For now, unlike his strike partner.

Vija Celmins, the Ultimate Artist’s Artist, Gets Honored by LACMA

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

On Wednesday, I walked down Crosby Street and stopped at an easy-to-miss building where, high above the cobblestoned street, is the studio of Vija Celmins, who’s been there for almost 50 years. It’s a classic gigantic SoHo artist loft, the kind you’ll be shocked to hear still exist—but they do. In the 1970s, the previous tenant put a Buddhist temple in the front room. Not finding anything particularly wrong with that, Celmins lived in the temple for a while.

“Then I finally thought, gee, maybe I could put a couch in here,” Celmins told me as I walked in.

Read True Colors: Inside the global deals and colorful critiques that make the art world go round, with a weekly guide to the must-see openings, auctions, and fairs.

It’s been decades since the galleries moved from SoHo to Chelsea and got replaced by boutiques. But among this warren of cast iron buildings, some of the artists who moved in during the pioneer days remain. Among them is Celmins, the masterful pop-landscapist who paints in infinite textures, taking on the universal with an obsessive tenacity. The 88-year-old is a living link to a long-gone generation, and someone always in the running when we talk about the best artists who are both still with us and who began their careers before the JFK assassination.

Approaching nonagenarian status, Celmins is still incredibly quick, with a generous, buoyant voice. She immediately hopped down the stairs of her old studio and took me over to the windows.

“I used to be able to see down the street before they built this ugly pink building—all the way to the bridge, because this was a parking lot,” she told me, referring to a building erected 21 years ago.

She walked past a print she made some years ago: a depiction of the ocean, her most famous subject, installed on the wall. She admired it in a refreshingly objective way.

“It’s so loose. Usually I tighten everything down,” she said. “I mean, it’s such a relief. I had forgotten that. And what did I do? I only made 10 of them, which is interesting, in ’16. I must have done this at… It looks like it’s a drypoint—doesn’t it look like drypoint? I mean, I did a fucking lot of oceans, as you know.”

Though not a household name, in certain circles, Celmins has attained near-mythic status. The very few people I told about this interview ahead of time were shocked she was having anyone over to her studios in Manhattan and Long Island. She works incredibly slowly, and thus has shown incredibly infrequently. This has started to change in the last few years. She had a retrospective at the Met Breuer in 2019, and in the last few years, a few shows of new work at her gallery, Matthew Marks—the blue-chip commercial concern that represents a disproportionate number of the greatest American artists.

Next year, Celmins will have a show at Dia Bridgehampton. Tomorrow, she’s the guest of honor at the LACMA Art+Film Gala, a big deal in American museumdom. It comes with a gigantic bash presided over by Leonardo DiCaprio and Eva Chow. The gala’s also honoring Denis Villeneuve, which Celmins is excited about. She’s seen the Dune movies and, like Villeneuve, is a big fan of the desert.

During my visit, she opened up a random drawer under a drawing table. There was stuff everywhere, decades of sketches and notes and Post-its. She has a constellation of postcards taped to the wall, with paintings as inspiration, ones that look nothing like Vija Celmins—Picasso, Balthus—along with photos of her heroes and her friends with pets. There’s Jasper Johns with his dog, Borges with his cat. She’s got framed artworks from friends who are mostly dead now, like a Ken Price drawing of some undulating cups—“You know Kenny Price?”—and a very kooky, absolutely amazing H.C. Westermann of a wolf with its heart throbbing in its chest, wandering through the desert. Westermann made it for her. She made Westermann a wolf drawing in return. She doesn’t know what happened to her wolf.

There are drawings of globes, sculptures of globes, her tablet paintings, more prints of the ocean, a paperweight from an insurance company in Kansas City—near where she grew up in Indiana after leaving Latvia—a wrapped-up canvas labeled “ED MOSES” on the side, and plenty of drawings of Saturn.

She moved into the SoHo place after two decades working in Venice Beach, California. While in LA, she honed and refined, and inarguably mastered, a painstakingly laborious practice of applying graphite to paper in slow, methodical marks, rendering the passage of time in hyperreal landscapes. She now mostly works in a studio on the eastern end of Long Island, where she's had a place for the last few decades. But it’s here at the SoHo studio, in the erstwhile cauldron of cutting-edge American art, where she loves to be.

“The studio out in the country is more like where I went back to painting, really,” she told me. “And here I did all these weird things.”

This wasn’t the first time we had met. A few weeks back, I was admiring the very cool Gary Hume show that Marks had up in his gallery on 24th Street when gallery director Jacqueline Tran tapped me on the shoulder and asked if I would like to meet Vija Celmins. Vija Celmins! At the public opening!

We chatted for a few moments. She seemed spry, fit, with an elastic mind. Then she invited me over for lunch—not in the city, but at her place on farmland out east. On an October morning I hopped on a Jitney, then an Uber to Celmins’s part of the hamlet, which is essentially rural, with small clapboard homes encircled by crop formations.

I arrived at the same time as my fellow visitors: Michael Govan, the director of LACMA, and his wife, the fashion executive Katherine Ross. Celmins welcomed us in and introduced us to her cat, Raymond, who immediately pounced on my cup of takeout coffee.

“Down, Raymond! He’s very friendly,” Celmins said. “He’s my son.”

Out here, in the two-room studio attached to her house, she’s been able to conjure a vision of landscape perhaps more potent than the breakthroughs she came to so many years ago. In her studio, she showed us how the raindrops on her window, as captured in her work-intensive flattened plane panorama, could take on epic qualities.

We sat down for lunch—quiche and soup from a local bakery—and Celmins started telling stories about California. She hadn’t lived there in a number of years, though the topography of the state is what willed her artwork into being. She drove out to the desert with James Turrell, with Doug Wheeler, and spent years—decades—arguing with Robert Irwin, who taught her at UCLA. Irwin called Celmins his “star student.” I’ve always liked this Bob Irwin quote about Celmins: “Vija has perfect taste. She doesn’t like anything.”

Govan told her about the museum, LACMA, and the new building that had finally been completed earlier this year after two decades of planning and fundraising. Celmins was quite curious about it all. She has a few plans for when she’s in town for the LACMA gala. She seemed intrigued about meeting her fellow honoree, director Villeneuve, and watched the Dune movies in preparation. (I heartily recommended she watch Sicario and Arrival as well.) She’s going to visit her old studio at 701 Venice Boulevard, which she hasn’t seen in decades.

“I was going to go out in the desert, but I’m not going to have time to do it,” she said. “I might have to make another trip. I really like the Death Valley desert because it’s so vast, so terrific. And they have airplanes zooming from the Air Force around really fast. I used to wave at them. They go really low, and they just go like, vvvvvrrroooooom! Right above, following the outline of the mountains.”

She’s also going to see Riko Mizuno, her Los Angeles art dealer, who is currently 94 years old. But most of her friends are gone.

“A lot of them have died, I mean, I didn’t have that many friends, but some died, and some are dying right now,” she said. “And then they moved away. Ed Moses, I knew pretty well. Billy Al Bengston. Well, Kenny Price, I loved Kenny.”

Then she mentioned Robert Therrien—“You know Bob Therrien?”—the late artist who has become quite famous for oversized sculptures that are astronomically popular on Instagram. A retrospective at The Broad this year became something of a sensation. I can’t imagine an artist more different than Celmins—but as it turns out, they had a deep mutual respect for each other.

“I met him once, and he was a very unusual person,” Celmins said. “He was really tall, and everything in his studio was like—he had a ceiling that was maybe like 25, 28 feet tall. And he had this little ladder that went up this side to this kitchen, which was this kitchen he remade from Chicago or someplace where his family used to live. I’ll show you one little thing. He gave me this.”

She got up from her chair and found the Therrian artwork on a shelf. It was a snout, bobbing up and down in a terrifying manner, attached to a curved metal rod. Just the inner part of the face of a canine, floating forever.

“It’s so disorienting. Where’s the rest of the dog?” I asked.

Roberto De Zerbi reveals Tottenham right-back conundrum in new Pedro Porro injury update

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Tottenham, featuring team news and expert analysis from Sam Tabuteau

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Roberto De Zerbi has admitted he has yet to decide whether to restore Pedro Porro to the starting lineup ahead of Tottenham’s trip to Leeds.

The Spaniard was named on the bench as Spurs made it three wins in a row with a 2-0 victory at home to Crystal Palace last weekend.

De Zerbi said after the game that the decision not to start the right-back, who recently returned to fitness after a month out with a hamstring injury, came from the player himself, who told his manager that he did not feel comfortable playing from the off.

Porro had initially injured his hamstring after insisting he was ready to start Spurs’ 3-2 defeat to Aston Villa in September before coming off injured after just 19 minutes.

De Zerbi was prepared to listen to his vice-captain ahead of the Palace game, even though he said post-match that he believed Porro was fit enough to start.

The Italian seemed confident that there were no issues with his full-back last weekend but he was reluctant to confirm whether he would be available to start at Elland Road.

“I have not decided yet about Pedro Porro, but it is not a tactical decision,” the head coach said.

“When Pedro Porro is fit, he plays because he is a top player, top guy, leader, clear leader, without problems.”

Porro has started just three of Spurs’ first nine league games this season, leaving Archie Gray to deputise in his absence.

The versatile Englishman has carved out an important role for himself in De Zerbi’s side and was promoted to the team’s five-man leadership group at the start of the season.

Gray leads the Premier League for underlapping runs and has made more sprints than any Spurs player this term, with De Zerbi describing him as a “very smart player”.

“Archie Gray is one of the best players in this moment, as a condition, as a confidence. He is playing very well,” De Zerbi continued.

Mikel Arteta laughs off Arsenal criticism amid Declan Rice injury concern

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Mikel Arteta has dismissed the suggestion that Arsenal are not managing Declan Rice's minutes carefully enough.

After playing 69 matches for club and country last season, the midfielder has again hit the ground running at the start of this campaign.

The midfielder has started all nine of the Gunners’ Premier League matches and has played the full 90 minutes in four of the last five.

Rice also started against Slavia Prague in midweek, while the likes of Martin Odegaard and Riccardo Calafiori dropped out of the starting lineup.

He played the full match despite looking extremely tired in the second half and will now join up with England, having missed the last camp due to load management.

However, Arteta issued a firm response when it was put to him that there could be concerns over Rice's workload.

He said: "Concerns? The only player, the only player in this squad that for two games in the Carabao Cup has been left out of the squad. At home with two days off. That's my concern.

"It's not true. We are really looking after him more than any other season. The way he's training and all the load. That's it.

"Here are the demands of this football club and you have a player like him. Not only him because there are many others that have even less time. That's it.

"That has to be the mindset. If you start to put other things in your brain, damage. Erase them, get them in the bin and just focus on what you have to do."

Arteta was just as forceful in insisting his side can have no excuses at the end of an unusual run of fixtures.

David Moyes Named the 4 Greatest Players He’s Ever Managed - Chose 2 Everton Icons

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

David Moyes has enjoyed a highly respectable managerial career over the years. Although his trophy cabinet might not be the most impressive – with West Ham United's Europa Conference League triumph in 2023 representing his biggest achievement – his remarkable longevity in the game deserves plenty of recognition.

Having managed in the Premier League since his appointment at Everton in 2002, he has established himself as one of the most successful managers in the competition's history, collecting more than 200 victories in the English top flight. After spells with Manchester United, Sunderland and West Ham in England, as well as a short period at Real Sociedad, he eventually returned to Everton in 2025.

Throughout his time managing several high-profile clubs, Moyes has worked with an abundance of outstanding footballing talent, including some of the greatest Premier League players of all time. Asked to identify the finest players he had managed by BBC Sport in 2024, the Scotsman named four individuals.

Wayne Rooney was a hugely gifted youngster who first came under Moyes' guidance at Everton before making his high-profile switch to Manchester United. The pair experienced some difficulties along the way, with the manager even taking the forward to court over a libel claim following criticism. Nevertheless, their relationship eventually improved sufficiently for them to reunite at Old Trafford.

Reflecting on Rooney's extraordinary ability, Moyes explained:

“Wayne Rooney: I think when I look back over the years, to have someone at such an early age be so talented, you know, known worldwide throughout his career, breaking Manchester United goalscoring records, breaking the England record for goalscoring… I think Wayne would have to be the standout player.”

When Moyes arrived at Manchester United, he inherited an exceptional defensive partnership featuring Nemanja Vidic and Rio Ferdinand. The squad also contained promising English centre-backs Phil Jones and Chris Smalling, but the former Everton boss clearly believed the Serbian defender, one of the greatest defenders in Premier League history, was in a class of his own, explaining:

"Vidic, I thought, was probably the best centre-half I'd worked with, I think I'd worked with a few at the time..."

From tough to downright terrifying, the hardest players of the Premier League era have been ranked.

Turning to a more recent example, Moyes' decision to highlight Declan Rice is hardly unexpected. The midfielder played a pivotal role in West Ham's Europa Conference League success in 2023 before completing a lucrative £100m transfer to Arsenal. Moyes clearly admired the England international's development and had plenty of positive things to say about him:

"At West Ham, for example, I had Declan Rice, who was another really young player, but one who was certainly on the rise and went on to fulfil his potential as a young player. I see him probably becoming an England captain in the near future.

Arguably the most unexpected inclusion among the four, Moyes appeared particularly pleased with the progress and achievements of Everton favourite Leon Osman. The former manager took great satisfaction from helping the midfielder earn international recognition, especially considering it came during the latter stages of his playing career:

"I think with people like Leon Osman, who was really really good player for us and went on to get an England cap when he was over 30, and sometimes those things give managers bigger thrills than some of the other boys."

Mikel Arteta is confident Bukayo Saka will make a successful recovery from his quad injury, with the Arsenal winger showing “some very good symptoms”.

Saka has missed the four matches the Gunners have played since he limped off in the opening ten minutes against Bayern Munich last month.

The initial expectation at the club was that the winger would be out for up to eight weeks, though scans were difficult due to the amount of fluid that had built up around the affected muscle.

The champions face Hull on Saturday in their final match before the international break and they will then return in a fortnight to start a heavyweight set of fixtures.

Between the end of November and Christmas, Arsenal face Borussia Dortmund, both Manchester clubs, Real Madrid, testing trips to Newcastle and Brentford, and a north London derby away from home.

Saka appears likely to miss many of those. A clearer timeline for his return will emerge after the international break, when Saka is expected to return to individual training on the grass.

When it was put to him that Saka could be out for at least another month, Arteta agreed but offered a positive update on how the early stages of the recovery have gone.

"I think so but I don't know,” Arteta said.

”The way it happened, it wasn't a really aggressive action. So that gives you some hope as well that the tissue will repair in a more efficient and faster way as well and he has some very good symptoms right now. So we have to wait and see."

Saka had made a bright start in that match against Bayern before picking up the injury in fairly innocuous fashion.

He burst away from Alphonso Davies but then collapsed to the ground as he played a pass out wide under no pressure.

Asked if Arsenal had got to the bottom of how and why Saka suffered the injury, Arteta replied: "Yeah, there are a few things that we believe can contribute.

“I prefer not to mention that here. But in reality as well to put a finger on something, maybe after you are totally wrong because there was something else. So it's very difficult.

“We have obviously thought and think and openly discussed a lot of things even with him to understand what could possibly have gone wrong. But the reality probably is that unfortunately we don't know.

Being ‘Good at Social Media’ Isn’t Enough to Run Your Business Accounts. Here’s What Is.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Invest in training your team, and your social media stops being a question mark and starts becoming one of your strongest business assets.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Small businesses do this every day. They hand off the Instagram social media account to a niece because she’s “always on her phone,” or have the youngest person in the office to run the social accounts by default. It’s understandable, and honestly, it’s a start! But managing social media for a business is a whole different ballgame. It takes real strategy, platform know-how, consistent execution and a clear understanding of what actually drives growth.

Here’s what I’ve learned working with clients across all kinds of industries: enthusiasm without training only gets you so far. Organic social media is one of the most powerful tools your business has, and it only delivers when the person running it knows what exactly they are doing. Here are seven reasons why training your social media team is one of the smartest moves you can make.

Paid ads are great, but organic social media is where your brand actually lives. It is how people discover you, decide if they trust you and choose to spend their money with you. Done well, it builds community, drives traffic and keeps you top of mind without a single dollar in ad spend. When I work with clients at Fix Your Search, one of the first things I do is help their team understand just how powerful this tool is, because most people underestimate it until they see a solid organic strategy in action.

That kind of impact does not happen by accident. It requires strategy, consistency and someone who understands how each platform works. Training is what gets you there.

This is the big one. Scrolling TikTok every night does not make someone qualified to manage your brand’s presence. Personal use and professional strategy are completely different skill sets. When I start training a client’s team, we talk about the difference between posting for fun and posting with purpose. Once that clicks, everything else starts to fall into place.

Here is the tricky part a lot of business owners do not talk about. If social media strategy is not in your forte, how do you teach it to someone else? You cannot spot mistakes you cannot see. When a client brings me in, the business owner does not have to be the expert. I take their team members from the very beginning, walking them through what good social media management actually looks like, step by step, so there is no guesswork on anyone’s end.

What works on Instagram is not what works on LinkedIn. What thrives on TikTok might flop on Facebook. Each platform has its own audience behavior, content format and algorithm logic, and not knowing the difference is one of the fastest ways to waste your team’s time and energy.

Platform-specific training is a core part of how I work with my clients. We always start with the fundamentals: understanding each platform’s purpose, writing captions that earn engagement, building a content calendar that makes sense and reading analytics without the eyes glazing over. That foundation is everything.

The algorithm loves relevance. Trending formats, sounds and topics get shown to more people, plain and simple. But jumping on a trend blindly without understanding how to make it work for your brand can hurt more than help. That is why trend awareness is a dedicated part of the training I do with my clients. When a team knows how to work with trends, reach can grow fast and it is always exciting to watch it happen.

Training is not one single afternoon session. Social media platforms change constantly, and a strategy that worked six months ago might be hurting your reach today. When I train a client’s team, I stay involved over time. We review what is working, adjust to algorithm updates and keep building on what the team learns. Everything I do is tailored to the specific business, because what makes sense for a local bakery looks completely different from what makes sense for a nightclub or an upscale restaurant.

Your social media accounts are often the very first impression a potential customer has of your business. That’s why, when I train a client’s team, I always tie the work back to this bigger picture. We’re not just filling a content calendar. We are crafting a presence that earns trust, attracts the right people and reflects the business at its best.

When proper training is in place, the results speak for themselves. Engagement climbs, reach grows and the team member who once second-guessed every post starts owning their role with real confidence. Watching that shift happen is honestly the most rewarding part of the work I do.

If your social media is not delivering the results you hoped for, the answer is almost never to scrap everything and start fresh. More often than not, the missing piece is proper training. Organic social media is a long game, and every business that plays it well has one thing in common: a team that genuinely knows what they are doing, why they are doing it and how to keep getting better. That kind of expertise does not appear overnight, but it is absolutely buildable. Invest in training your team the right way, and your social media stops being a question mark and starts becoming one of your strongest business assets.

Small businesses do this every day. They hand off the Instagram social media account to a niece because she’s “always on her phone,” or have the youngest person in the office to run the social accounts by default. It’s understandable, and honestly, it’s a start! But managing social media for a business is a whole different ballgame. It takes real strategy, platform know-how, consistent execution and a clear understanding of what actually drives growth.

Here’s what I’ve learned working with clients across all kinds of industries: enthusiasm without training only gets you so far. Organic social media is one of the most powerful tools your business has, and it only delivers when the person running it knows what exactly they are doing. Here are seven reasons why training your social media team is one of the smartest moves you can make.

Paid ads are great, but organic social media is where your brand actually lives. It is how people discover you, decide if they trust you and choose to spend their money with you. Done well, it builds community, drives traffic and keeps you top of mind without a single dollar in ad spend. When I work with clients at Fix Your Search, one of the first things I do is help their team understand just how powerful this tool is, because most people underestimate it until they see a solid organic strategy in action.

Derided Thierno Barry is no longer a source of frustration as star fires Everton to victory over Coventry on another big night for David Moyes amid Jack Grealish drama

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published: 17:29 EDT, 6 November 2026 | Updated: 18:44 EDT, 6 November 2026

Maybe Everton should request to play under the lights every week. It certainly seems to push David Moyes' team to new heights.

Following hot on the heels of consecutive night victories over Newcastle United, Friday's 3-1 win against Coventry City was a reminder of why, under Moyes, there is renewed sense of optimism at Everton.

Up to fifth, above Liverpool, and in a Carabao Cup quarter-final at Fleetwood, it could prove to be a special season.

This always felt like a big task for Frank Lampard, back at Everton for the first time since his sacking in 2023.

Popular as the ex-manager may be, there's no room for sentiment, certainly not from Moyes on a night he took charge of his 592nd game, drawing level with Harry Catterick for managing the most games in the club's history.

He'll set it outright after the international break against Brentford and Keith Andrews, who was 21 when Moyes first took up the reins at Everton.

Thierno Barry (right) now has 10 goals this season after being widely derided in his first season on Merseyside

Kiernan Dewsbury-Hall had been left out of the England squad in the morning, but refused to let it spoil his night

His side started impressively, fashioning three shots in the opening 10 minutes, before losing all momentum and allowing Coventry into proceedings.

It took going 1-0 down, to a soft Yann Gboho goal, for the hosts to come alive. Thierno Barry, so often the source of frustration last season, was magnificent and all but won the game with a five-minute brace in the second half.

The striker didn't score his first goal until December last season. This campaign, he's reached 10 before the second international break. Moyes joked post-match that Erling Haaland should watch his back in the Golden Boot race is starting to feel less absurd. Barry now sits joint second in the charts.

Both goals against Coventry came via back post-duels. The first he confidently headed past Carl Rushworth, before bundling in a second five minutes later.

The disgruntled home fans booed the team off at half-time, yet were jubilantly cheering and signing after Barry's brace. The Frenchman danced on the pitch at full-time and was serenaded by the South Stand.

'I don't see loads of young centre-forwards scoring loads of goals in the Premier League,' said Moyes.

'He's come in and has settled in a bit more. His language and communication is better and obviously he's worked to get himself into the positions to score.'

Barry wasn't the only Everton goalscorer, of course. Kiernan Dewsbury-Hall was not one of the three Everton players named in Thomas Tuchel's England squad on Friday - only Arsenal had more representatives.

EVERTON (4-2-3-1): Pickford 5; Rohl 5 (O'Brien 73min, 6), Tarkowski 5, Branthwaite 6 (Keane 89), Mykolenko 6; Armstrong 6 (Hackney 73, 6), Garner 6; Dibling 5 (Johnson 73, 5), DEWSBURY-HALL 8, George 5 (Alcaraz 90); Barry 8.

COVENTRY CITY (4-2-3-1): Rushworth 4; van Ewijk 5, Mfuni 6, Pinnock 6, Dasilva 6; Yirenkyi 6, Torp 6 (Grimes 86); Sakamoto 5 (Tchaouna 86), Rudoni 5 (Hamer 86), Gboho 6 (Thomas-Asante 73, 5); Awoniyi 5 (Cherif 87).

But the midfielder sent a pointed message to the Three Lions' boss with a superb showing that was capped with a late powerful goal to end the contest.

'It was a disappointing morning, I'm not going to lie,' Dewsbury-Hall told Sky Sports after the game. 'It's a massive dream to play for my country. I know I was close.

'Yeah, sad, but really proud of myself and how I have reacted. All I can keep doing is putting in good performances and hopefully one day I get the nod, because I'm never going to give up. I've got the mentality. I'll keep pushing and pushing and see what happens.'

'He's desperate to play for England,' added Moyes. 'You never know if England get a couple of injuries, or anything changes.'

What ended as a comfortable win could have been a different outcome had Jame Tarkowski been punished for a hair pull on Caleb Yirenkyi, or if Vitalii Mykolenko's tussle with Taiwo Awoniyi in the box had been deemed a foul.

Both were checked and cleared by VAR, as was a handball claim in the build-up to Barry's second goal.

'Tarky pulls on Caleb's hair, they [VAR] are saying it's not enough of a pull,' said an aggrieved Lampard afterwards. 'That's a hard area to be in. What is hair pull and what's not?

'I think Taiwo, on the edge of the box was a penalty. I feel there's a penalty by the time Myko's knee is in the back of Taiwo's knee on the line of the box.

'Then the Barry one is handball. He flicks it in [with the hand] with the final touch. Nothing went for us tonight.'

Thierno Barry shows his prowess again as Everton storm into Premier League's top five

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Everton 3-1 Coventry City: The forward’s form as the club’s only recognised striker is proving a major boost. Only Erling Haaland has scored more than him in the League

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Everton climbed into the top five after coming from behind to claim a 3-1 win over Coventry, powered by more goals from Thierno Barry.

The 24-year-old’s double flipped the contest against former Toffees manager Frank Lampard’s side. Only Manchester City forward Erling Haaland, with nine, has registered more league goals this season.

Barry’s unpolished moments can create frustration, demonstrated in the first half when he wasted a clear shooting opening by failing to sort his feet out, but he proved effective inside the six-yard box after the restart.

Having fallen behind to Yann Gboho’s first goal for the Sky Blues on his second league appearance, the Frenchman scored a header to equalise before adding a second by scrambling the ball in from barely two yards.

Lampard will feel his team should have received at least one penalty after James Tarkowski appeared to pull Caleb Yirenkyi’s hair while the game was goalless in the first half, though Coventry’s defensive work left much to be desired.

It is the first time Barry has scored in three straight matches for Everton, and only the second time in his career.

He has now scored six in his last seven games across all competitions and 10 for the season, two more than he recorded during his inaugural campaign.

His display aligned with pre-match remarks from boss David Moyes, who equalled Harry Catterick’s record of 592 matches in charge, having stated that "Erling Haaland should be worried".

And while realistically the Norway international will not be losing any sleep at the thought, Barry’s form as the club’s only recognised striker is proving a major boost.

With Kiernan Dewsbury-Hall chipping in with goals from midfield, scoring the third nine minutes from time, to add to his all-round creativity Moyes is squeezing everything out of his limited resources.

A third successive win also took the focus off the circus surrounding Jack Grealish.

He has been absent for the last four matches after being disciplined by the club for missing training, but he returned to Finch Farm earlier in the day to begin a tailored programme which is likely to see him return to the first-team fold after the international break.

A nondescript first half which began with Everton in the ascendency finished with the visitors in command and was notable mainly for Tarkowski’s tangle with Yirenkyi as they challenged for a cross.

His grasp of the midfielder’s hair was so slight it appeared to go unnoticed by everyone except the player and no action was taken by VAR.

Coventry’s second penalty appeal was checked by the officials but Vitalii Mykolenko’s challenge on Taiwo Awoniyi was ruled to have been made fractionally outside the box.

But when the ball bounced around the area after the visitors were able to play their way from right to left Gboho, surrounded by three Everton players, poked through a shot which appeared to take Pickford by surprise as it crept over the line.

The lead lasted four minutes as Barry barely had to get off the ground to head home Mykolenko’s cross after Jay Dasilva crumpled to the floor.

Within five minutes they were ahead as he again outjumped Dasilva at the far post to bundle home off his midriff with a panicked goalkeeper Carl Rushworth unable to keep it out.

Dewsbury-Hall made the result safe with a fine angled finish to displace Merseyside neighbours Liverpool in fifth place.

Worker or queen? A genetic advantage may shape a fire ant’s future

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A study of fire ants suggests a “selfish” supergene influences body size, potentially giving some ants a better chance of becoming queens.

Most red imported fire ants become workers. A few become queens, the largest ants in the colony and the ones that lay eggs.

How large an ant grows during development helps determine whether it becomes a worker or a queen.

A new study suggests that genes also influence this process.

In colonies with several queens, ants carrying a particular version of a large group of genes were more common among the biggest workers and future queens.

Haolin “Horace” Zeng and Brendan Hunt, insect researchers at the University of Georgia, studied this pattern in lab colonies.

Their work offers a clue to why these genes keep spreading, even though they can have drawbacks for the ants carrying them.

Some fire ant colonies have just one queen laying eggs, while others have many. A group of more than 500 genes helps determine which kind of colony develops.

These genes sit close together on a chromosome, a structure that carries DNA. They pass from parent to offspring together as a package called a supergene.

“We call them supergenes because the genes are tightly linked and inherited together, almost like one big gene,” explained Hunt, an associate professor in UGA’s entomology department.

The package comes in two versions. Scientists call the older version B and the newer one b, which appeared about one million years ago.

Ants in single-queen colonies have two copies of B, written as BB.

In invasive populations with several queens per colony, every egg-laying queen has one copy of each version, written as Bb.

Queens with the Bb combination weigh less and have more trouble starting colonies alone than BB queens. Yet the b version continues to spread.

Scientists describe it as a “selfish genetic element” because it improves its own chances of reaching the next generation, even when it does not help the ant carrying it.

Becoming a queen gives a female ant the opportunity to pass her genes to the next generation.

“If you want to pass down a gene, you’d better become a queen. You don’t want to become a worker,” Zeng said.

Young ants grow as larvae before becoming pupae, the stage when they change into adults. A natural chemical called juvenile hormone can delay the move out of the larval stage, giving them more time to eat and grow.

The researchers wanted to find out whether ants with the b version responded differently to this hormone.

In spring 2024, they collected colonies with one queen and colonies with several queens from Clarke and Oconee counties in Georgia.

They used these ants to set up small lab colonies without queens.

Each held about 2,500 workers, and all colonies received the same total weight of young ants at different developmental stages.

Some colonies received one meal containing methoprene, an artificial chemical that acts like juvenile hormone and is also used as an insecticide.

The others received a meal without it, and workers passed the food to the larvae.

Between 15 and 24 days after that meal, the team collected and weighed worker pupae. The middle weight, or median, was about three times higher in treated colonies than in untreated colonies.

The researchers then checked the genes of 1,049 worker pupae and 195 queen pupae from colonies with several queens. They compared the ants’ genes with their weight.

Ants carrying the b version became more common as body weight increased.

Here, ‘carrying the supergene’ refers specifically to the b version.

“Among the smallest workers, about half carried the supergene,” Hunt said. “That proportion increased to roughly 65% among the largest workers and 75% to 83% among queens.”

On average, BB pupae weighed about 19% less than Bb pupae. But the heaviest ants in each group weighed about the same, so the b version did not raise the upper limit on size.

Instead, ants carrying b reached the larger sizes more often.

The hormone treatment increased size by about the same amount in both groups, suggesting that a major difference in their response to this hormone did not explain the pattern.

The share of Bb ants also rose with weight at a similar rate among workers and queens.

That suggests the gene package affects body size, which then helps shape whether an ant becomes a queen, rather than having a separate effect on queen development.

“To me, it highlights that, through a developmental mechanism involving body size, a genetic element can alter caste outcomes and basically cheat its way into making a queen,” Hunt said.

The team next looked for genes that might explain the size difference. Scientists do not yet have a full record of which genes become more active as fire ant larvae grow into workers or queens.

The researchers therefore used existing data from pharaoh ants, which belong to the same ant subfamily.

Earlier work on adult fire ants had identified 191 genes that were more active in Bb ants than in BB ants.

Of those, 34 were also more active in pharaoh ant larvae developing into queens. That overlap was larger than the researchers would expect by chance.

One gene, called gas8, appeared in every comparison and sits within the fire ant supergene.

DNA near the start of gas8 is also more loosely packed in Bb fire ants, which fits with the gene being more active.

The team still does not know how the gene package changes growth.

Perhaps large BB larvae die more often before becoming pupae, or perhaps some Bb larvae spend longer eating and growing before moving to the next stage.

A computer simulation showed that extra growing time could produce the pattern seen in the study. Giving Bb larvae a better chance of continuing to a larger size could also explain it.

Workers might treat larvae carrying b more favorably, but that alone seems unlikely to explain the findings.

An earlier experiment found extra Bb queen pupae even when only BB workers raised them.

Researchers have not yet tested what gas8 does as ants develop. Experiments with fire ant larvae will be needed to find out.

Comparisons with other animals that carry supergenes could answer a wider question: do these gene packages often spread by changing how animals grow, or how long they spend growing?

The study is published in the journal Proceedings of the Royal Society B.

What Runners Need to Know About Rhabdo

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

After a tragic death in the running community, doctors want runners to know the risk factors for the condition.

Shortly after the New York City Marathon, tragic news broke: A 24-year-old runner, Rachel Apa, collapsed on the course and died in the hospital after doctors worked for six hours to save her life, according to the New York Times. Apa’s father told NYT the cause of death was rhabdomyolysis (a.k.a. rhabdo).

The news is shocking and saddening—as well as unsettling. Questions are popping up from runners on social media as to how something like this could happen to a young woman who was reportedly a former Division I field hockey player at Bucknell University and had spent months training for the marathon.

To help answer some of those questions, we talked to doctors to explain what runners need to know about rhabdo, and how to stay safe.

First, know that rhabdo is relatively rare and dying from it is even rarer. Approximately 26,000 cases of rhabdomyolysis are reported annually in the United States, and the mortality rate starts at 10 percent but may go up to 50 percent in severe cases.

In short, rhabdo is a breakdown of muscle that in and of itself is not life-threatening. In fact, Derek M. Fine, MD, a nephrologist at Johns Hopkins Medicine, says that if you were to test creatine kinase (a.k.a. CK, a blood marker of muscle breakdown) in marathon runners after the race, “most” of them would show elevated levels of the enzyme.

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention says that multiple CK tests are the only way to accurately identify rhabdo, though. Normal CK is between 20 and 200 U/L, but rhabdo is diagnosed when it reaches five times the upper limit.

“There’s always a little bit of muscle breakdown and the reason is because, at least in [a] marathon, you’re using the muscles so much that at some point they can run out of an energy supply,” Fine says, adding that the mitochondria can't keep up and make enough of what’s called ATP (adenosine triphosphate), which is your primary source of energy.

Rhabdo becomes dangerous when it becomes more severe. As muscle tissue breaks down, the body can release harmful substances into the bloodstream, including myoglobin, a protein that can damage the kidneys, and excessive amounts of potassium.

As for myoglobin, the protein can get “stuck” in the kidney and cause kidney failure or injury at various levels, Fine says. “It can be quite severe, but that usually takes a little bit of time to manifest,” he explains.

Fine says that high levels of potassium are probably a bigger risk than myoglobin. “When the muscle cells break up, you get a very large release of potassium into the bloodstream and that potassium then can cause cardiac arrhythmias,” he says. This can cause kidney failure (your kidneys are the organ tasked with getting rid of extra potassium) or potentially sudden cardiac arrest.

“Under normal circumstances, if it’s happening slowly and you’ve got decent kidneys, you pee the potassium out and it never gets that high in the blood,” he says. “If there's severe rhabdo and you have a huge amount of muscles breaking down, you can release a ton of potassium and your body can't get rid of it fast enough.”

In addition to the rapid release of these substances, rhabdo can sometimes lead to other issues that resemble sepsis or even multisystem organ failure, Fine says.

Still, rhabdo is not a common underlying cause of cardiac arrest, says Lili Barouch, MD, director of sports cardiology at Johns Hopkins Medicine and an endurance athlete herself. However, it can lead to abnormal heart rhythms due to severe electrolyte problems, primarily high potassium. And as Fine also explains, kidney failure (as well as other organ failure) can result in cardiac problems, she says.

It’s not just running that can increase risk of rhabdo—any heavy exercise can do damage to muscles, Barouch says, particularly exertional rhabdomyolysis. In fact, Fine says that there have been incidents of rhabdo reported in CrossFit athletes for this reason. Whether it’s a marathon or weight lifting, it’s in high-effort situations that the muscle breakdown can accelerate rapidly and lead to problems.

As for running, while training does induce microscopic damage to muscles that later repairs in a stronger way—a positive outcome—many people push harder in a marathon. “For a race, you’re usually going at a much higher level of effort and you’re really pushing yourself to your limit,” Barouch says.

This can be true for athletes of any level, but people who aren’t as highly trained who push really hard on race day may be at higher risk, Fine says. “The most common reason [for getting rhabdo] is you’ve got someone who overdoes it and who wasn’t trained enough,” he says. (This was not necessarily the case with Apa, just a potential risk factor for runners in general.)

Another risk factor is dehydration, Barouch says. “If someone’s dehydrated, the amount of those muscle breakdown products [your body produces] that can become toxic to the kidneys starts to really go up. You're unable to clear the toxins out of your body fast enough and that can cause more damage,” she says.

On the flip side, good hydration can help flush the myoglobin from the kidneys, per Fine. But, he points out that there’s a delicate balance between being over- and underhydrated: “While you’re running a marathon, your kidneys are getting messages from your brain to hold on to water, so if you keep putting water in there, the water dilutes out the sodium in the blood and you can get hyponatremia,” he says. The key: Drink to thirst and take electrolytes during a long or hot race, Fine says.

Carbohydrates could help as well. “Part of rhabdo could be if you’re not giving enough carbohydrates and energy [to your body], you can't make ATP [the energy currency you need to perform] and that’s where the muscle could break down,” Fine says.

Another factor for marathon runners is environmental conditions. “If it’s a particularly hot day, for example, that seems to be a big risk factor,” Fine says. In fact, there was an unusually large incidence (24 cases) of exertional rhabdomyolysis at the 2022 NYC Marathon, per a study in Nephron Clinical Practice, likely associated with the “unseasonably high heat and humidity” on race day, per the authors.

Younger runners may actually be at higher risk of rhabdo, likely because they’re more inclined to take risks and push harder than the older population, Fine says, potentially ignoring signs of rhabdo.

There’s also a genetic component to rhabdo, Fine says. So, if you have had rhabdo before, or have a family member who has had rhabdo, it could be worth looking into your own risk more closely.

It’s not super common, but genetic tests look at certain markers that might suggest someone is more susceptible to rhabdo. “It’s not like you're going to say, ‘Okay, I’m going to run a marathon, I’m going to go do prescreening genetic testing,’” Fine says. “But in someone who may have had this before, even if they said, ‘Well, I did CrossFit and I got rhabdo and I'm now going to try to run a marathon,’ that sort of person may be worth checking.”

Finally, certain medications (including statins and certain antidepressants) and underlying conditions (like McArdle disease and Duchenne muscular dystrophy) might predispose someone to rhabdo, Barouch says.

The experts agree that signs of rhabdo can be hard to identify because aches are pretty much unavoidable when running a marathon. Still, severe pain in the working muscles (e.g., the legs) is a red flag, Fine says, as are muscle weakness and swelling.

“Of course during a marathon, people tend to have muscle pain, but if it’s much more severe than usual or much more severe than expected,” that’s what to watch for, per Barouch. Severe muscle soreness after the race is also something to keep an eye on, she adds.

Another thing to look out for is urine color. “What one might see early on is very sort of dark orange, rust-colored urine, and that’s the myoglobin coming out,” Fine says. “If someone’s got muscle pain and their urine’s orange, they probably want to go and get checked out and maybe get hydrated and get a CK level checked in an emergency room.”

Per the CDC, the earlier you get treated, the more likely you’ll recover without long-lasting or permanent health effects, so don’t wait if you suspect you have rhabdo.

At the end of the day, it comes down to listening to your body. “As endurance athletes especially, we tend to push ourselves really hard for a race because we have personal goals, time goals, things like that, but you still have to listen to your body,” Barouch says.

If something feels off, don’t ignore it. “Marathons are not comfortable to run for anybody, but if your muscles seem extremely painful or if you start to have other symptoms, if you’re having breathing problems, chest problems, things like that, that seem out of the ordinary, [do] not ignore those warning signs either for rhabdo or another medical condition that might be building up,” Barouch says.

Why the Henley Is Fall’s Most Unexpected Trend. Here’s Why You Should Care

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Consider this your sign to rethink the most underrated shirt.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

THEY’VE BEEN CROPPING up everywhere. All over my social media feed, featured across men’s runway collections at Fashion Week, seen on Hollywood heartthrobs like Michael B. Jordan, and so forth. If you haven’t figured it out by now, I’m talking about the henley. This once-overlooked wardrobe staple has found new popularity, showing no signs of slowing down as it becomes one of fall’s most surprising menswear trends. And quite frankly, I’m here for it.

The henley hasn’t traditionally been the sexiest shirt in a guy’s wardrobe. For years, it’s been the tee you’d throw on for a lazy Sunday or wear underneath a flannel. But somewhere along the way, the fashion crowd decided it deserved another shot. Its roots go all the way back to 19th-century England, where it started as an undergarment before becoming a go-to for rowers in Henley-on-Thames, hence the name. Who would’ve thought a shirt with such humble beginnings would eventually become a fashion obsession on my Instagram timeline?

Cos Slim Ribbed Cotton Long-Sleeved Henley T-Shirt

Todd Snyder Lightweight Mini Waffle Henley

Cherry Los Angeles Waffle-Knit Cotton-Blend Henley T-Shirt

California Arts Murray Slim Long Sleeve Henley

Here’s what I love about the henley: It does more heavy lifting than your average T-shirt without trying to be anything fancy. You get the simplicity of a crewneck, the button placket of a polo, and just enough texture to make an otherwise basic outfit feel put-together. And while the classic cotton version is still very much in rotation, ribbed, waffle-knit, and thermal styles are giving guys even more ways to wear it. If you need further proof that the henley is having a moment, just take a peek at the runways. For Fall/Winter 2026, Paul Smith sent a model down the runway in a white henley tucked into wide-leg black trousers, while Isabel Marant took a more layered approach, pairing a light blue henley with a navy corduroy jacket.

I’m a firm believer that proportions can make or break an outfit, and the henley is no exception. My favorite way to wear one is with baggy jeans or relaxed trousers. The contrast between a fitted top and roomier bottoms creates a flattering silhouette. And if you’ve spent the past few months putting in work at the gym, a henley is one of the easiest ways to show off those gains without looking like you’re showing off. The closer fit naturally accentuates your chest, shoulders, and biceps, especially when you leave the top button or two undone. Just remember, there’s a fine line between fitted and too tight.

Whether you’re dressing one up with trousers and loafers or keeping things casual with loose jeans and boots, the henley proves that sometimes the most unexpected trends are the pieces we’ve had in our closets all along. And it’s about time this one received its flowers.

The AI posting war is heating up: 'Please, leave me behind,' says artist who got into it with Epic CEO Tim Sweeney on X

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Your weekly update on everything you could ever want to know about the games you already love, games we know you're going to love in the near future, and tales from the communities that surround them.
