Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"The Blacklift rocket is the springboard to significantly expand our total addressable market."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Another new private rocket is in the works.

AST SpaceMobile announced today (Nov. 6) that it's building a reusable rocket called Blacklift to launch its broadband satellites to low Earth orbit (LEO), with first flight targeted in 2028.

The news comes just 10 days after Cowboy Space unveiled Big Iron, a rocket designed to launch its constellation of 20,000 AI data centers. Cowboy Space is also targeting 2028 for its first liftoff.

"The Blacklift rocket is the springboard to significantly expand our total addressable market, providing AST SpaceMobile with regular unconstrained access to orbit and providing for most favorable owners’ economics in the industry," Abel Avellan, the company's founder, chairman and CEO, said in a statement today.

"This will accelerate our mission to build a new layer of global connectivity, extending connectivity beyond terrestrial networks and helping to bring AI-enabled services wherever people live, work and travel," Avellan added.

Texas-based AST SpaceMobile is building a LEO constellation composed of its BlueBird satellites, which beam internet service directly to cell phones. The company has launched 13 BlueBirds to date, including eight "Block 2" variants. The antennas on each of these next-gen spacecraft cover a whopping 2,400 square feet (223 square m) — the largest ever deployed in LEO by a commercial satellite.

BlueBirds have flown atop three different rockets to date — SpaceX's Falcon 9, Blue Origin's New Glenn and India's Launch Vehicle Mark-3. But AST SpaceMobile wants to get more vertically integrated, which explains the push to develop Blacklift.

The company won't be starting from scratch. It has signed a deal with another Texas-based outfit, Firefly Aerospace, to provide the engines (and associated engineering services) for the first three Blacklift launches. After that, AST SpaceMobile will build the engines itself, under a licensing agreement with Firefly.

Those engines are Miranda, which will power Blacklift's booster, and Vira, which will power the upper stage.

Neither engine has flight experience. (Firefly's currently operational rocket, Alpha, uses Reavers in its booster and Lightnings in its upper stage.) But Miranda has been tested extensively on the ground, firing for more than 4,200 seconds over the course of 175 hot fires, according to AST SpaceMobile.

"The Miranda engine will allow us to leapfrog the traditional five-to-10-year development cycle as we build Blacklift," AST SpaceMobile President Scott Wisniewski said in the same statement.

The "supply and services" agreement for the first three launches is worth $500 million. The licensing agreement cost AST SpaceMobile about $800 million, with half of that paid in cash and half in stock, according to the statement.

Blacklift will be able to haul 20 tons to LEO. Each launch will therefore be able to loft up to five BlueBird satellites, according to today's announcement. (For perspective: The Falcon 9's LEO lift capacity is about 25 tons.)

BlueBirds will continue going up while AST SpaceMobile gets Blacklift ready to fly: The company already holds contracts with other providers to launch more than 100 of the satellites over the course of 2027 and 2028.

The launch landscape will likely be very different when Blacklift, Big Iron and other new rockets come online. SpaceX has said that it plans to phase out the Falcon 9, as well as its Falcon Heavy heavy lifter, after its Starship megarocket gets up and running.

That milestone could come in the next few years, ushering in something of a launch crunch for satellite operators. SpaceX has indicated that it will prioritize launches of its own satellites with Starship. And there will be a lot of them to loft — 100,000 Starlink V3 satellites and a million Starmind AI data centers, company founder and CEO Elon Musk has suggested.

How ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Used Real-World Sound to Make Its Monsters More Terrifying

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

No one is doing monster movies quite like director Takashi Yamazaki and his team — who not once but twice now, with “Godzilla Minus One” and “Godzilla Minus Zero,” have made Godzilla feel both bigger than Tokyo Bay and extremely emotional and personal, even handcrafted. Much of that comes down to the narrative balance between our human heroes and the King of the Monsters, of course. But it’s also about the grounded, physical ways the monsters are brought to life.

Much has already been made of the fact that both Godzilla’s and King Ghidorah’s roars were recorded by blasting the sound effects against real physical spaces (and probably scaring some hikers in the process) so we wouldn’t just hear something otherworldly. We’d hear the texture of the loudspeakers, the reverberation of the sound on the concrete of a dam (for Ghidorah) or a dome (for Godzilla) — the unreal brought into a real, physical space. Related Stories ‘Zi’ Review: Kogonada Rediscovers His Voice with a Microbudget Hong Kong Experiment Starring Michelle Mao and Haley Lu Richardson ‘To Hold a Mountain’ Took on the Government — Now It’s Montenegro’s Oscar Submission

But that is only the light-up blue tip of the kaiju for the work done by the “Godzilla Minus Zero” sound design team. Sound designer Natsuko Inoue and production and post-production sound mixer Hisafumi Takeuchi constantly found ways to tie the most fantastical moments back to Earth, because that sonic believability keeps the audience invested in the action.

“ When audiences hear sounds that they’ve never heard before too frequently, I think it removes you from the world because it’s too foreign and it’ll stand out,” Inoue told IndieWire. “Once a fantasy-like or fictitious sound goes through the sort of real-world echo, then it becomes a lot more palatable, or we become more receptive to how it sounds because it’s echoing in a way that we recognize. So that was a way that we really tied the visuals to the sound and made it feel more grounded.”

This grounding process is especially important, and especially challenging, when things that should be on the ground take to the air as a part of Ghidorah’s gravity beam. Inoue did an initial pass of the sound effect for the beam that allows Ghidorah to throw whole houses up in the air high enough for our favorite eternally tormented, former Kamikaze pilot Kôichi Skikishima (Ryûnosuke Kamiki) to fly past them. But it wasn’t quite right.

“So I went back into the Foley studio, and I wanted to capture that feeling of something being lifted and rising. I took this very small speaker, and I placed it right next to a bunch of different objects — a sheet of metal or some wood or a water tank — to capture a sound that almost felt distorted,” Inoue said.

That distortion, bounced off different types of objects, captured the sensation Inoue was after: the sonic equivalent of your stomach lifting into your mouth.

“Oftentimes in sound design, when you layer too many sounds or put too much processing on it, it starts to ruin the original material or the original sound that you’ve captured. So this distortion became a very strong foundation. I didn’t add too much post-processing on it,” Inoue said.

What she did do was hand it over to Takeuchi to create the sense of whole blocks caught up in Ghidorah’s gravity beam and preserve the dialogue and add background dialogue, so that we’re always afraid for the human beings in danger during Ghidorah’s rampages.

When Shikishima first flies through Ghidorah’s gravity beam, the sound team really wanted to lean into the character’s emotional response to something that’s mindbendingly horrible. “I added a Doppler effect — so like when an ambulance or a police car drives by and the sound shifts slightly — [to give] the sensation that you’re passing through,” Takeuchi told IndieWire.

It’s that shifting of sound, more than any one particular sound, that adds a scale to the horror beyond what Shikishima or what we can see. “We wanted to be very mindful of the balance and what it was doing and what it was supposed to do for each scene,” Takeuchi said.

“What we were trying to do is take sounds that you would hear in nature and then design and add to it in almost like an extension type of way, and that’s how we make the Kaiju as well as the gravity beam feel more real,” Inoue added.

“Godzilla Minus Zero” is now playing in theaters.

Letterboxd’s Sale to the New York Times: 9 Pros, Cons, and Big Questions from the $330 Million Deal

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

After several months of business talks and speculation, Letterboxd is about to have new owners. The New York Times is closing in on deal to acquire the film-logging social media platform, which values the company at $330 million. It’s an eye-popping increase from 2023, when the Canadian holding company Tiny bought a majority stake at a $50 million valuation.

Far beyond the film industry, there is a dismal track record and daunting uncertainty when it comes to companies’ ability to maintain their identities after being acquired. Social media reception to the pending New York Times deal generally peaked at resigned relief (“the best case scenario” was a common refrain) while also drawing more cynical, even existential responses about the future of Letterboxd. As the website’s user base holds its breath over the future, these are some pros, cons, and big unknowns to consider about where Letterboxd is going. Related Stories Letterboxd Finalizing $330 Million Sale to The New York Times ‘The Weed Eaters’ Lands Expanded Release from Yellow Veil Pictures: New Zealand Cannibal Movie Sets Winter Rollout

There’s understandably a lot of negative social chatter about the news of Letterboxd’s looming acquisition, but let’s rest assured that it’s at least not a studio taking ownership of the wildly popular cinephile engagement platform. A24, Sony, Netflix, and Skydance were all reported to have registered talks about an acquisition. Whether or not this new iteration of Letterboxd will exist behind a paywall is one matter, but that’s not the worst thing compared to the potential studio-ification (i.e. A24-ification, as they were in talks for the sale) of the brand. Compare it to studios running an exhibition chain — not just Sony purchasing Alamo Drafthouse, but all the way back to the days before the Paramount Decrees, which legislatively blocked studios from having a monopoly on theaters. (Decrees that are no longer in effect.) In that situation, there’s more potential for marketing nepotism and favoritism to titles and properties in-house. A media company taking over Letterboxd should, in theory, prevent the kind of self-interest that threatens independence. —Ryan Lattanzio

A deal would bring two unusually loaded cultural conversations under one roof. The movie-logging platform has helped reshape the world’s repertory film scene — effectively gamifying the enjoyment of archival art — while debates about the Times often wind their way back to the paper’s coverage of Palestine and trans rights. For Letterboxd users who see that coverage as having real consequences for whose lives and experiences are taken seriously by mainstream journalistic institutions, the prospect of the Times owning a community they helped build raises questions of trust. A bigger audience would be a genuine upside, but for some users, who owns the room is an existential issue. —Alison Foreman

While some users might not even know about Letterboxd’s digital magazine, the Journal has been a growing presence on the platform, with a staffed editorial team regularly publishing interviews, retrospective considerations, film festival coverage, and other longform articles. The question remains on how, or if, those writers and editors will be integrated into the New York Times newsroom. A shuttering of the Journal would wreck yet another publication for professional film coverage and freelance opportunities, of which there are fewer and fewer. On the other hand, when the New York Times bought The Athletic in 2022 for a whopping $550 million, the banner initially continued as a semi-independent banner. But a year later, the Times dissolved its existing sports department, laying off or reassigning staff, and positioned The Athletic as a replacement. The New York Times’ culture desk has not been immune to shake-ups over recent years; the potential addition of Letterboxd begs the question of whether further changes are in store. —J. Kim Murphy

As the number of Letterboxd accounts has skyrocketed over the past few years, navigating the platform has become a bumpier experience for users. Pop-up Cloudflare turnstiles reading “Verifying you are human. This may take a few seconds” make frequent interruptions clicking from page to page, while Gremlins-centric error messages signaling server crashes are semi-regular occurrences. The New York Times draws much more web traffic than Letterboxd and such technical hiccups are essentially anomalous. The company certainly has the resources to bolster the strength of Letterboxd’s servers. —JKM

Letterboxd users have historically been an extremely discerning audience, sensitive to changes that might be seen as minute or inconsequential for other websites. A memorable hiccup from recent years came when the platform abruptly removed an option to list out all star-ratings given by the accounts one follows on a single web page. After some backlash from users (like myself) who noticed the feature’s disappearance, Letterboxd was swift in reintegrating the tool. It was a small crisis with a happy ending, but it speaks to how quickly UI changes can dishearten the platform’s users. If the New York Times wants to make even small tweaks to the platform, or heavily integrate it into its digital ecosystem, it will face an audience that’s quick to rally against such changes. —JKM

The New York Times Company is the poster child for brand elasticity in the digital media age. America’s newspaper of record didn’t just adapt to the Internet; it built an empire of games, recipes, and sports that a sizable chunk of the world needs to engage with in a daily basis. But it’s less apparent how Letterboxd fits into the strategy. The social network exists somewhere between an editorial product and a game and doesn’t have a clear comp within the NYT organization. Is the plan to simply move the entire Letterboxd infrastructure behind the NYT paywall, making the $30 monthly subscription tier even more unavoidable for cinephiles? Draw on users’ data to bolster revenue opportunities for streaming promotions and the Times’ product recommendation banner Wirecutter? The answer might be some combination of the above, but the Times is entering uncharted territory. —Christian Zilko

Plenty of filmmakers IndieWire has spoken to are concerned about the widening gap between what defines film criticism and what defines a hot-take social media sound-off, i.e., the “instant reaction.” Reviews on Letterboxd can range from the thoughtful and long-form to the pithy and snarky. As film critic jobs evaporate globally, what happens when you merge Letterboxd closer to the purview of established, institutional film writing, the kind that’s hard, that demands a chunk of your soul? Letterboxd has further democratized criticism, no question, and while many of us argue that we need an elevated discourse, what happens if more people start to think… we don’t? Will the fusion of the Times and Letterboxd stoke that debate, or create harmony? Will this only fuel the growing primacy of influencers over critics and journalists as an authority on a film’s quality? Either way, cantankerous conversation will abound. —RL

It follows then that if enough Letterboxd users distrust the Times, they might log fewer movies, post fewer reviews, or exit the app altogether. From a web-development perspective, the basic mechanics of a movie diary are incredibly easy to replicate. (Hell, AI just cloned Adobe Photoshop.) The hard part is getting the consumers whose aggregate taste is a cultural bellwether to once again show up in the same place. It wouldn’t take a mass exodus for Letterboxd to feel fundamentally different, and if the core group of people who reliably made consumers laugh or put weird repertory picks on your radar stop contributing, there will be fewer reasons to engage. The community could splinter across new competitor sites — which, yes, opens up potential opportunities for aspiring critics to gain exposure and find their followings. But we can’t know if any of that would be as big as the OG Letterboxd. —AF

I’m a Deals Writer, and These Are My Top 10 Tech Deals This Week

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

As Lifehacker's deals writer, I'm always looking for the best bargains on TVs, speakers, laptops, and other tech. Now that it's Friday, I've gathered together this week's sales highlights, all of which I've vetted using my favorite price-tracking tools to make sure they are actually good deals.

This week, you can already feel the early Black Friday deals heating up with price cuts on the M5 MacBook Air, Bose speakers and headphones, a Samsung OLED TV, and more.

The MacBook Air with the M5 chip comes in both 13-inch and 15-inch sizes, and if you're interested in either, it's a good time to buy: Amazon is selling both right now at a $200 discount. That brings the 13-inch MacBook Air to $1,099.99 (down from $1,299) and the 15-inch MacBook Air to $1,299 (originally $1,499), reaching their lowest prices since the June price hikes.

The WH-1000XM5 headphones were at the top of their class until they were supplanted by the XM6s. On the plus side, by virtue of being older, they're currently discounted to $198 (originally $399.99). These are still incredible headphones in 2026, and with a $200 discount, they are a steal.

Bose’s second-generation QuietComfort Headphones are down to $199 from $359, saving you $160 and bringing them to their lowest price yet. Weighing around 8.1 ounces, they’re among the lightest premium noise-canceling headphones, with generously padded earcups and a cushioned headband that spreads pressure without gripping too tightly. They’re also easy to pack for travel, as the earcups fold and swivel for storage in the included hard-shell carrying case.

The Bose SoundLink Plus hasn’t been on the market for long, but it’s already earned strong reviews—CNET labeled it “the Goldilocks of Bose Bluetooth speakers,” and it’s down to $179 (originally $269). This is a great opportunity to snatch a quality portable speaker with a big discount.

Amazon has cut the price of its latest Fire TV Stick 4K, dropping it to $24.99 from $59.99 and bringing it to its lowest price ever. At this price, it's an affordable way to bring 4K streaming to an older television or give a sluggish smart TV a performance upgrade.

The Sony WH-CH730N are $128 (originally $179), the lowest price they've ever been. They use the same Sony Connect app as the premium headphones; as a result, you get a 10-band slider on the EQ menu, a battery care feature that extends the longevity of your battery life, 50 hours of juice, ANC, Ambient Sound (transparency mode), Multipoint to connect to two devices at once, and Bluetooth 6.0.

I got the Roborock Qrevo CurvX because it seemed like the perfect robot vacuum for me, as I have a dog, a mix of different floor types, and a gym with a workout mat, and it excels at handling them all. Amazon currently has it for $749.99 (originally $1,499.99), a 50% discount, and the lowest price it has ever been.

Samsung’s HW-Q600F soundbar is down to $379.97 at Amazon from $499.99, a $110 discount that brings it to its lowest price ever. This 2025 model sits in the middle of Samsung’s Q-Series lineup and pairs a sleek, angular bar with a subwoofer in a 3.1.2 setup. Dolby Atmos also brings a modest sense of height, and effects track cleanly from left to right. You get a graphic equalizer and sound presets for adjusting the audio to your preferences through the included remote.

The Ultimate Ears Miniroll is down to $49.99 (originally $79.99), the second-lowest it has sold for so far. It’s a small, flat, puck-shaped Bluetooth speaker that weighs just over half a pound, with a removable rubber strap you can attach to a backpack, bike handlebar, or shower caddy. Despite its size, the Miniroll can get surprisingly loud—which PCMag confirms in its review—and at moderate volume, it has enough sound for a kitchen or backyard without sounding thin. As for its battery life, you get about 12 hours of playback before it needs a charge over USB-C, while its IP67 rating protects it from dust and a brief dunk in water.

If you're looking for a premium model, Samsung’s 65-inch S90H OLED TV is down to $1,397.99 from $2,697.99 at launch, saving you over $1,000. The sale price is easier to justify when you consider that this is a new 2026 model. If the 65-inch model does not suit your space, Samsung also sells the S90H in 42-, 48-, 55-, 77-, and 83-inch versions, and those sizes are discounted as well.

Ryan Serhant Reveals the Turning Point That Made Him a Real Estate Star—and How He Thinks Agents Can Cash In on AI

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Before Ryan Serhant became a recognizable face in real estate, he was one of thousands of agents showing up to an open casting call—and declaring himself the best in the universe.

The opportunity would put him on “Million Dollar Listing” and turn him into what he described as a “walking billboard,” giving him a platform he was determined to make count.

Speaking at NAR NXT 2026 in New Orleans, Serhant reflected on that career-changing break, the relentless social media strategy that followed, and why he believes agents should now be looking to artificial intelligence to help build their businesses.

His message to agents navigating an uncertain market was straightforward: “Markets don’t dictate your income. They dictate your strategy.”

For Serhant, learning where to direct his energy was part of that process. He recalled spending too much time early in his career stressing about things he could not control, including what might happen in the months ahead.

Instead, he encouraged agents to focus on the work they can tackle now. The future will arrive regardless, and worrying about it does little to move a business forward.

"One of the mistakes in the beginning of my career was stressing about what I can't control," he said. Agents should "attack" what they can control "without worrying about the future."

Branding himself a "futurist," he added, “At the end of the day, no one knows what they are doing."

Serhant acknowledged that his first few years were difficult and suggested that getting started as an agent is even harder today. His own breakthrough came after he attended an open casting call for “Million Dollar Listing” in 2010, joining roughly 3,000 real estate agents in Times Square.

He recalled not fully knowing what he would be doing there. But when asked why he had come, he delivered an answer that left little room for self-doubt: “I’m the greatest real estate agent in the universe.”

Serhant revealed the season was filmed with four people, but only three made the cut.

Once the show aired, he recognized that the exposure had given him an opportunity to distinguish himself—and he felt compelled to capitalize on it.

“Once the show came out, I became a walking billboard,” he said.

He also acknowledged the role timing played in his rise. He was finding his footing professionally as both cable TV and social media offered opportunities to reach potential customers.

“I need to meet my customer where my customer is,” he said.

That approach led him to embrace Instagram in its early days, posting photographs of listings with himself in the images. He recalled posting every two to three hours, steadily building an audience that could become familiar with him before ever meeting him.

The goal was to establish a presence on people’s phones, creating recognition that could carry into an eventual conversation or business relationship.

Today, Serhant said, helping other agents succeed has become a source of excitement in its own right.

“It makes me tick. I enjoy it. I love it,” he said of teaching others.

Having encountered a wide range of challenges throughout his career, he described watching his agents win as the work that now gets him most excited.

He also emphasized his belief in investing in the future, describing Serhant’s mission as helping the world move forward.

For agents considering what that future looks like, artificial intelligence was a central part of his advice.

“The excitement within AI isn’t just language. It’s in creating work,” he said.

Serhant encouraged agents to identify administrative tasks that technology could reduce, freeing up time for the activities that depend on human connection and creativity.

“Humans should be doing only what humans do, which is meeting and creating,” he said.

He pointed to negotiation support and homeownership information as potential uses, while urging agents to think beyond individual tasks and consider how AI could help generate more business.

The measures that matter, he suggested, include increasing transactions per agent and improving gross profit. In that framing, the value of adopting AI lies in what it enables an agent to accomplish.

But he also shared an anecdote illustrating how the technology can influence a client’s decisions.

Serhant recalled a buyer pulling out of a $50 million deal after asking ChatGPT whether they were paying too much and receiving an affirmative answer.

The account underscored a challenge for agents: Clients can now arrive with AI-generated conclusions that affect their willingness to move forward with a purchase.

His advice was to use the technology as support in their own work.

Across his reflections on TV, social media, and AI, Serhant returned to the importance of approaching opportunities with a willingness to act.

“I learned that optimists are successful," he said.

Kelsi Karruli is a senior celebrity reporter who joined Realtor.com in 2025. She writes about trends encompassing real estate and celebrity properties. She previously wrote for Daily Mail, True Urban Culture, and JMedia Corporation, where she specialized in covering celebrities, relationships, psychology, and gender issues. She has a bachelor's degree in journalism from Pace University, where she received the James Rose Award for excellence in journalism. iOS 27.3: New features, release date, more

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This week, Apple released the first iOS 27.3 beta. The update includes multiple new features for iPhone users, including the redesigned Health app, iPhone Duo updates, and much more. Here’s everything you need to know about what’s new in iOS 27.3 …

As my colleague Ryan explained, Apple will likely release iOS 27.3 to all iPhone users sometime in December. Typically, Apple would release its x.2 software updates in December, but this year is different because iOS 27.1 was exclusive to iPhone Duo. That means that, in terms of the timeline, iOS 27.3 is essentially the equivalent of iOS 27.2.

Here’s when Apple has released its December updates in recent years:

So with that in mind, we expect iOS 27.3 to be released sometime during the first half of December.

The redesigned Health app is back in iOS 27.3. This was first available during testing of iOS 27.2, but did not ship as part of that final update.

The redesigned Health app is split into three tabs: Insights, Longevity, and Browse. The app features an all-new design that dynamically adapts based on your data, including Apple Intelligence. The Longevity tab includes a new Health age feature that uses Apple Watch data to show how your metrics track relative to your actual age.

For Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4 users, it also offers easy access to view your Readiness score every morning.

For Apple Fitness+, iOS 27.3 adds a new Gym Strength feature. This creates progressive, four-week plans based on your strength goals, experience, schedule, and available equipment.

As you complete each week of workouts, the following week adapts based on the reps and weights you logged.

For iPhone Duo users, iOS 27.3 adds Reachability support. This setting shifts the interface on the outer display to the right or left to make it easier to reach the far corners. It’s designed to make iPhone Duo easier to use with one hand, despite the wider outer display. You activate it by swiping right on the right edge of the screen or left on the left edge.

Reachability for iPhone Duo is on by default. You can turn it off in Settings > Accessibility > Touch > Reachability.

iOS 27.3 also tweaks the iPhone Duo’s software keyboard by making the keys slightly narrower and the font slightly smaller.

When it debuted, the iPhone Duo included a more robust version of StandBy with new clock faces and expanded customization options. iOS 27.3 brings the iPhone Duo’s StandBy experience to all iPhone models.

This includes the redesigned Analog and Digital Clock faces, as well as additional customization options for colors and complications. The update also adds support for Shared Albums to the Photos face.

iOS 27.3 brings several key Siri AI updates. First, the update expands Siri AI with many new capabilities across Apple apps. Apple explains:

We started rolling out these new capabilities across many Apple apps, including Calendar, Files, Finder, Journal, Mail, Maps, Messages, Music, Notes, Photos, Preview, Reminders, Safari, Shortcuts, and Weather, so users can do things like find a coffee shop they visited last week or archive files that haven’t been opened in over a year.

There are many more actions available now as examples for you to reference. To see what you can do in each app, just ask Siri AI.

With iOS 27.3, Apple is also starting to test new ways for developers to create Siri AI integrations for the first time. This is described as an “early Developer Preview.” Apple explains:

Today’s preview allows any app to bring its content and actions directly into Siri AI by using App Intents, making it easier for all apps to tap into Siri AI’s newest capabilities, including personal context understanding, onscreen awareness, natural language, and more. So people can just ask for help in their own words, and Siri AI carries out any custom action you’ve built, from checking a delivery to booking a reservation to placing an order.

While this is being tested as part of iOS 27.3, Apple says it will start shipping to customers in a few months.

iOS 27.3 also expands Siri AI to new languages, including French, Japanese, Korean, Portuguese, and Spanish.

iOS 27.3 brings the original set of Apple Intelligence features to China for the first time. This does not include Siri AI or other iOS 27 updates, but it does include the following features:

iOS 27.3 is a major update to Safari. First, the update adds support for a new vertical tabs design layout on iPhone Duo’s inner display. If you choose this option, your Safari tabs will be arranged vertically along the left-hand side of Safari, rather than along the top. Vertical tabs are also available on Mac and iPad with macOS 27.3 and iPadOS 27.3.

iOS 27.3 also adds a new Adaptive Dark Mode feature. This feature can automatically apply Dark Mode to websites that don’t natively support it. You can choose to have Adaptive Dark Mode automatically kick in or use it on a site-by-site basis.

For Apple Music users, iOS 27.3 adds a new “Selfie Sing” feature that lets users record a video of themselves while singing karaoke. To use this feature, start playing a song and go to the lyrics view. Then tap the new camera button that appears on the right-hand side of the lyrics.

The update also refines the design of viewing lyrics while your iPhone is in landscape mode.

A Much-Requested Battlefield 6 Feature Revealed Last Spring Won’t Arrive In 2026 After All

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Platoons were added back in Battlefield 3. They functioned essentially as MMO-style clans, helping players form communities and keep track of one another across larger groups than just their immediate party. EA confirmed the feature would be returning in Battlefield 6 when it dropped its 2026 roadmap back in April. This week, however, it said the feature will stay MIA until 2027.

“Platoons are arriving during Season 5 and will focus on the foundations of forming a social group in Battlefield 6. Players will be able to create or join Platoons of up to 100 members, easily party up with other members, and represent their Platoons with their tags,” the publisher announced earlier this year. “Platoons will also support different join settings with leadership tools to help manage membership. Players who play together with their Platoon will also be able to earn XP bonuses.”

Some fans had previously been worried that the feature might be gone from the franchise forever, alongside a number of other additions to Battlefield 6 the community has been waiting on. Platoons were set to go live in early December alongside a server browser and new map updates. In fact, as recently as October 21, EA was still reiterating that they would arrive in season 5. Yesterday, however, the developers confirmed the feature won’t ship this year after all.

“We remain committed to delivering high-quality experiences for our players,” read a status update posted on X. “We have determined that the B-81 Cavalry and our Platoons feature are not ready for deployment as they do not meet our internal quality standards.” More details on the revised roadmap for 2027 will be shared “after the holidays.”

Today during ExileCon's keynote address Grinding Gear Games announced that Path of Exile 2's full release on December 11 will bring not one, but two new classes: Alongside the previously announced Duelist, 1.0 will also introduce the dagger-heavy, trap-wielding Shadow, capable of employing a suite of new stealth mechanics.

The Shadow's addition had been hinted at with a cryptic teaser trailer, which you can see here. The class's inclusion is welcome news to those of us—like yours truly—who very much enjoy playing these sneaky stabbers. On release, Shadows will be able to ascend as the returning Assassin, or choose a new ascendancy: the Infiltrator.

We know that eventually, every PoE2 character class will have (at least) three ascendancies. Whether the Shadow's third will be the Saboteur, the Trickster, or something new like the Sorceress's Disciple of Varashta is yet to be seen.

As has been the case with previous PoE 2 class additions, the Shadow's arrival will also introduce a new weapon type and abilities. In 1.0, alongside the Duelist's swords, we'll also be getting the dagger, and with it all new skill gems and supports. We should see an absolute explosion of playstyle archetypes on top of PoE2's already healthy build diversity.

The Shadow will introduce new mechanics like stealth, which prevents monsters from seeing you until you’re close by. It has a cooldown, but since it’s reset on kill, you should be able to use it to kill quickly from the shadows and get moving again.

Stealth interacts with the Shadow's returning trap skills, too. While PoE 1 traps were mostly just hucked at monsters' feet mid-combat, traps thrown from stealth in PoE 2 won't trigger until you reveal yourself, giving an actual feel of pulling off an ambush, There will be eight different trap types in 1.0, all of which interact with each other in unique ways.

There is, as ever is the case with Path of Exile, even more. When entering stealth, you leave behind a shade that blinds monsters and acts as a distraction. Your shade will also copy melee attacks you use, doubling up on your damage. And Shadows will be able to utilize a new Darkness resource, which can be collected from darkness-spreading skills to buff the damage you deal when emerging from stealth.

We’ll know more about these classes later, but between all these new abilities and ascendancies as well as two new acts, a new league, and endgame improvements Path of Exile 2’s full release is looking absolutely stacked.

Serie A | Venezia 2-1 Udinese: Kike Pérez stunner spoils Zanetti’s debut

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Kike Pérez hit a superb long-range winner as Venezia beat Udinese 2-1 at the Penzo, spoiling Paolo Zanetti’s first game as Udinese coach against his former club and sending the Friuliani to the bottom of Serie A.

Zanetti was appointed earlier this week after Kosta Runjaic was sacked. Udinese had taken one point from their previous eight league games and went into this relegation six-pointer one point above Davide Nicola’s Venezia, whose only win of the season had come against Napoli last month.

Venezia started on the front foot, and Antoine Hainaut forced an early save from Maduka Okoye inside the area. Nicolò Zaniolo then wasted a good chance when he found space in the box and shot wide.

The hosts kept pushing. Juan Jesus headed a corner just over and Pasquale Mazzocchi curled a long-range effort inches wide. At the other end, Filip Stanković kept out Jurgen Ekkelenkamp. Hainaut had the best chance of the half when he collected a rebound and fired over, and it stayed 0-0 at the break.

Udinese should have led early in the second half. Keinan Davis went one-on-one with Stanković inside the six-yard box, but the Venezia goalkeeper made a fine save to deny him.

It proved costly, as Venezia went ahead in the 54th minute. Hainaut crossed from the right and Toma Bašić finished low into the bottom corner.

The lead lasted only three minutes. Matías Moreno diverted the ball into his own net, and the equaliser stood after a long VAR check on Zaniolo’s position in the build-up.

Pérez restored the hosts’ lead in the 65th minute, picking up Mazzocchi’s pass and sending a left-footed shot from outside the box into the top corner, leaving Okoye with no chance.

Mazzocchi was forced off injured soon afterwards, and Udinese looked for a way back. Stanković saved well from substitute Giorgi Chakvetadze’s long-range shot. Venezia nearly made the game safe from a corner, when Moreno headed wide and Simon Sohm had a shot blocked, and Akor Adams had a goal ruled out for offside with nine minutes left.

The win lifts Venezia off the bottom, a point behind Monza. Zanetti’s Udinese drop to last place, and both sides return after the international break: Venezia visit Juventus on 22 November at 12:30 CET [11:30 GMT], while Udinese travel to Bologna at 15:00 CET [14:00 GMT].

What It’s Really Like Being the Third in a Threesome

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

“It kinda takes all of the pressure off of me.”

Although Michelai, 33 had been in threesomes before, she was typically the person in the relationship inviting an additional body to the mix. But for the first time, she was going to be the third—and she was thrilled by the idea of it.

She was approached by a married couple on a trip to Vegas. "They were seeking me out, which also made me a little bit more confident and comfortable,” she says.

When it comes to threesomes, conversations usually revolve around experimenting sexually when you’re already in a relationship: What boundaries to discuss, whether it’s the right decision for your and your partner, and what aftercare may look like. It’s rare that the third person in the scenario is addressed. But communication, attention, and care are required for everyone involved, including the person joining just for the night.

“A ‘successful threesome’ isn’t about fulfilling the couple’s fantasy—unless that is explicitly discussed, negotiated, and consented to,” sex therapist Dr. Ty David Lerman, PhD tells Cosmopolitan. “Rather, it’s about all parties showing up as equals with all needs, desires, pleasure, and boundaries being honored.”

And when you do it right, it can be really hot and fun, without all of the responsibility that may come if you’re the one in the relationship.

“It kinda takes all of the pressure off of me,” Jess, 30 says.

Jess joined her best girlfriend and her situationship at the time. At first, it was just to watch them (which she really enjoyed). But eventually it turned into a regular hookup with the both of them.

Stepping into an established couple’s world could feel awkward, but Michelai says it didn’t. Because she’d been the “primary” woman before, she knew which questions to ask. “I made sure there were no unspoken rules,” she says. “I laid everything out on the table, and they laid everything out on the table.”

She also read the room. The husband was clearly letting his wife lead, so Michelai directed her questions to the wife before she ever approached him. And the husband’s etiquette in the moment won her over: “I really respected that he would straight-up ask her something in the middle of us doing something, like whether he could do this or that. I was like, ‘You know what? Hell yeah.’”

Jess, meanwhile, felt like a bit of an outsider at first—but only because she wanted to give her friend time to adjust. Since the threesome was her idea, she didn’t want to overstep. “The first time I was with them, I just watched,” she says. Afterward, her friend and the situationship asked her: Why didn’t you touch yourself? Why didn’t you join? “I didn’t want to overstep.”

Since then Jess has learned how important communication is to being a third.

“Communication is probably just as important as the threesome itself,” Michelai says. “You need to know what everyone wants, what everyone’s comfortable with, and what everyone is expecting the experience to be.”

She’s heard the horror stories of couples that only sought out a third to save the relationship or where boundaries were drawn around the man’s pleasure in an MFF.

“There’s a term in the polyamory community called ‘unicorn hunters,’” Dr. Lerman says. It’s reserved for a couple, usually heterosexual, seeking a third, usually a woman, to fulfill a fantasy, usually the man’s. In practice, he says, this often means the couple using the third for their own purposes. “The third feeling objectified, used by one or both of the couple, disempowered to really consent to the role they are playing for the couple, and often leaving unsatisfied.”

His fix is a stoplight system: green means hell yes, yellow means needs negotiation, red means hell no. He recommends running through it before anyone gets near a bedroom, and using it again mid-action, because consent is ongoing and can be revoked at any time. Or just ask, “How is everyone doing?” Simple, and it works.

But this doesn’t mean there’s a set routine that’s going to play out in the moment. “There’s this expectation that threesomes are all three people going at it equally, but I think it can be something a little bit more defined than that,” Jess says. She and her friend had decided beforehand that they wanted to “use him like a toy,”—something he had no issue with—so the two women didn’t hook up with each other. The rules can be unconventional as long as everyone agrees to them.

And as unsexy as it may seem to have a lot of discussion, it really pays off during the sex.

“We had conversations about everything before we got in the bedroom, so when we got in the bedroom, everything felt really organic,” Michelai says.

Being a third may seem more “relaxed” than being in the couple inviting another person in, but there are still instances where it might not be a fit.

A major red flag is if it’s obvious that the couple hasn’t talked to each other. “A major tell for the third in vetting a couple is if the couple has already had these conversations before you enter the picture,” Dr. Lerman says. “If they haven’t, don’t walk, run! It’s a glaring red flag that they do not know what they want, and they don’t have the communication skills to talk about it together, which means they sure aren’t going to be able to do that with you either.”

Things like double standards, rules that only apply to you, no conversation about sexual health, or any action that may make you feel like more of an accessory than a person.

“Pay attention to how the couple reacts when you say no, or voice a need, or your own boundary,” he says.

Michelai saw the flip side of this when she was the primary. She and her boyfriend brought in one of her best friends from college. “He was hyper-focused on her for like 10 minutes, and that was the longest 10 minutes of my life,” she says. She left the room. (“Should I have left? Probably not.”)

For the person who goes home alone, aftercare can be an afterthought, which is exactly the problem. Couples often assume that once the sexual interaction is over, it’s fine for the third to just get up and leave. But how would that feel in any other sexual scenario? Not so great, right?

Michelai explains that the married couple made sure to make her feel welcomed—without any awkward obligation to feel part of the relationship. “We definitely had the Kiki, cuddle, laughs, yap session after each time we had sex, and I felt very comfortable.” The wife even asked for alone time with her, so the two ordered champagne and took a bubble bath while the husband slept. “We didn’t have sex again without him, but we were cute, cuddly, making out, and had a really nice bubble bath.”

And when it was over, it was over. “I think we all happily had a great experience and happily went our separate ways,” she says.

Jess’s first time was a different story. The guy got weird afterward. “He was like, ‘OK, I’m just going to go to bed,’ and didn’t invite us to stay the night or ask how it went or anything like that, which was very jarring.” She and her friend salvaged the night with a debrief on the car ride home: How did you feel? Did you like that? It was hot when he did this. Ideally, she says, everyone would debrief together, or at least one-on-one.

Dr. Lerman says aftercare should be negotiated upfront, like everything else. “Good aftercare looks like starting that conversation ahead of any play,” he says. Will there be a puppy pile of cuddling? A text the next day? A second date to debrief? “If we can’t consent to aftercare, we don’t play. Simple.” Being the third adds a layer: what happens when the couple goes back to being a couple and you’re outside it? “Aftercare is ultimately about making sure nobody feels disposable once the clothes go back on.”

“I think you can suss out the vibe and feel the vibe between the couple to know if it’s a safe space to join in on,” Michelai says. She’s flattered when couples approach her, too. “That actually makes me feel more confident.”

Jess’s criteria are simple: both people have to be into it. “It wasn’t just one of the partners trying to do something sexually adventurous.” And she wants to be treated like a person. “These are real people that you’re probably going to be having a sexual encounter with, and they should be treated as such.” Couples can get a little fetishistic about a third, she says, but you can weed that out quickly by seeing whether both of them want to sit and talk with you like a real human.

“The most common mistake occurs when a couple takes on the mindset of ordering up a sexual experience rather than approaching this as a negotiation with all parties,” Dr. Lerman says says. Opening with a laundry list of rules the third has no say in creates a power imbalance from the jump. He also warns against expecting equal chemistry all around, which rarely happens. Be conscious of everyone and bring them all into the play, but let attraction go where it goes.

A Canadian official backs the #SaveMassEffect movement

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Suddenly, the vibe around the #SaveMassEffect movement has shifted.

At first, it seemed like little more than a grabby hashtag allowing Mass Effect fans to have their voices heard after EA's shocking closure of BioWare and cancelation of Mass Effect 5 last month. But since then, the Change.org petition has racked up more than 300,000 signatures, and now the Canadian government is being forced to acknowledge the situation.

That's because a separate petition asking the Government of Canada for federal support for BioWare has far surpassed the amount of signatures required (3488 currently with a 500-vote threshold) to be certified and presented in the House of Commons. The rules of the Parliament say "the government must respond to every petition presented to the House within 45 calendar days of its presentation or filing with the Clerk."

The petition for federal support isn't about saving Mass Effect specifically, but securing financial support so that BioWare can go independent. It has three stated goals: "Provide BioWare with the capital necessary to secure independence of themselves and their intellectual property from Electronic Arts and resume business as an independent, Canadian company," "Protect Canadian businesses and cultural institutions from being dictated by American and foreign corporate interests," and "Champion the values of inclusivity, tolerance, democracy, justice, and civil rights at the centre of Canada's national identity by preserving BioWare's invaluable contribution to the global entertainment industry."

The fact that the Canadian government is now required to respond the petition is encouraging enough, and now the Member of Parliament representing the official request is voicing his support for BioWare, Mass Effect, and the overall movement online. In a scathing rebuke of EA's treatment of BioWare, New Democrat Parliamentary (NDP) leader Don Davies voices support for the Save Mass Effect movement explicitly and directs users to his Linktree, where the government petition is linked.

"BioWare is an Edmonton-based video game studio that created acclaimed series like #MassEffect, Baldur’s Gate, and Dragon Age," Davies writes. "Canada’s video game industry creates some of our most important cultural exports, and #BioWare was among our greatest success stories."

The developers at BioWare know how to run their studio. They know how to make the games that fans love. They should be given the first opportunity – and the federal support – to buy it and run it themselves. If you agree, sign the petition at the link in my bio! #SaveMassEffectNovember 6, 2026

"BioWare’s US parent company, EA, has decided to close the studio. These publishers buy developers, run them into the ground, and rip them apart for profit. There's a pattern of US companies buying Canadian businesses and shutting them down, costing Canadians their jobs.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

"The developers at BioWare know how to run their studio. They know how to make the games that fans love. They should be given the first opportunity – and the federal support – to buy it and run it themselves."

While this is all very exciting and validating for BioWare fans, it doesn't mean the studio is coming back any time soon. The NDP isn't in power in Canada currently and has lost its official party status with just five of the 343 seats in the House of Commons. And even if this petition somehow prompted coordination from the highest levels of the Canadian government, EA still owns the rights to BioWare's IP, and until it shows any willingness to sell anything off, which it's notoriously reluctant to do, fans are at its mercy.

Still, the dual petitions to save Mass Effect and get funding for BioWare are worthwhile endeavors, and the louder they become, the more likely it is that change happens, whether it impacts BioWare specifically or other game studios. And for that reason, I'm choosing to mentally file this as unequivocally good news.

Two incoming lawmakers plan to defend health equity from Trump administration cuts

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Anil Oza is a general assignment reporter at STAT focused on the NIH and health equity. You can reach him on Signal at aniloza.16.

Amid a sweep of Democratic victories in Tuesday’s midterms, some online commentators jokingly declared “Woke is back.”

There was little congressional pushback over the past two years as the Trump administration terminated hundreds of grants meant to chronicle and remedy medical inequities, shuttered programs intended to diversify the science workforce, and opened investigations into universities and medical schools it accused of discriminating against white and Asian applicants. Will that change now that the Democratic Party has won control of the 120th Congress?

Two newly elected candidates with science credentials told STAT they hope to change that: Michigan Senator-elect Abdul El-Sayed, an epidemiologist and former health disparities researcher, and incoming Rep. Jasmine Clark of Georgia, a microbiologist who will be the first Black woman with a STEM Ph.D. in Congress.

Democrats have a long list of priorities, and with President Trump still wielding his veto power, passing bills related to health equity will still be an uphill battle. The White House posted on X Thursday, “FYI: DEI is still dead.” But, the introduction of at least two lawmakers with personal connections to health equity work could offer a counterweight to such policies for the rest of the second Trump administration.

“I know firsthand what DEI initiatives can do for students who are trying to find their way, and they don’t actually quite know all of the options that are available to them,” Clark told STAT in an interview Wednesday. A former professor, she said such programs have opened up opportunities for her students in the sciences.

El-Sayed, interviewed a few days before the election, said, “The war on diversity, equity, and inclusion is about a vision of an America that doesn’t include certain kinds of people. I will always stand for an America where everybody is included and empowered, and I think that’s critical for us as we move to the future.” During his time in academia, he did research on disparities in maternal health and within the Arab American community. He added, “we need to be able to document the ways in which our systems are allowing for and, in some respects, creating the differences in the ability for people to live long, healthy lives. It’s important work, and it should help guide where public policy gaps are and where we need to fill them.”

The second Trump administration has seen unprecedented changes to the scientific ecosystem. Some of the most major have drawn forceful responses from Congress. Proposals to cut the National Institutes of Health’s budget, cap funding for overhead costs at universities, and exert more political control over federal funding have all been rebuked by Congress. With the Democrats taking control of both chambers, further pushback may be coming.

The NIH did not respond to a request for comment.

A number of physicians and scientists who won their races have noted that accountability for health secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. is high on their list of priorities. Part of that accountability should be understanding which communities and researchers were affected by policies like grant terminations over the past two years, said Jennifer Jones, who directs the Center for Science and Democracy at the Union of Concerned Scientists. “I want to know who was most harmed by this because there’s absolutely a justice and equity issue at play when you clearly attack issues of diversity, equity, and inclusion,” she said.

While equity work is sometimes portrayed as controversial, Philip Alberti, the founding director of the Association of American Medical Colleges Center for Health Justice, said polling he has done has shown broad support for health equity initiatives across ideological groups. “Three-quarters of U.S. adults support the goal of ‘all communities having a fair and just opportunity to be healthy,’ and 65% of adults who have ever heard the term ‘health equity’ support it, too,” he said. “This was true across all subgroups, including across groups defined by self-identified ideology. This is not controversial.”

Clark trained as a microbiologist before taking to politics during the first Trump administration. She said she would seek an assignment on the House oversight committee and has a number of questions she’d like to pose to appointees like Kennedy. For one, she would like to understand why the administration has repeatedly pushed for a controversial hepatitis B vaccine study in Guinea-Bissau. Clark likened that trial to the Tuskegee syphilis experiment, an unethical study conducted by the U.S. Public Health Service and the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention that withheld treatment for syphilis from nearly 400 African American men from 1932 to 1972.

Another way she has seen the fallout of the administration is that her former lab, which studies HIV transmission, had to pause some of its longitudinal studies in Africa because of restrictions on international collaborations. Even if the lab is able to find more funding, “you basically have to start from scratch, or you just have a big hole in your data. That’s not what we need to be doing if we want to actually do things like end the HIV epidemic,” she said.

She also lamented the process of grants for federal funding being screened for certain terms and phrases to determine if they are related to “DEI.” This process has particularly affected researchers studying health disparities, and has made it difficult for some to apply for new funding. In September, a handful of researchers sued the agency, arguing this process infringed on their first amendment rights.

“Having the ability to write in your research grant that you’re looking at the disproportionate impact without having to dance around those words because any grant that has the word ‘disproportionate,’ or ‘woman,’ or ‘Black,’ or ‘gender,’ or anything like that all of a sudden gets flagged,” she said. “That’s not the environment we should be operating under if we actually want to answer real-life questions. So I look forward to being a part of a Congress that will prioritize moving toward and not immediately dismissing research because they are trying to answer questions about why one group is impacted more than another.”

Anil Oza is a general assignment reporter at STAT focused on the NIH and health equity. You can reach him on Signal at aniloza.16.

Live Science readers respond to a recent poll about whether they'd visit the Red Planet even with potential health risks.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

For centuries, Mars has been seen as a potential place for future human settlements. But long-duration space travel comes with serious risks to astronauts' health, including those resulting from cosmic radiation and the effects of microgravity.

A new study found that rats that were exposed to radiation similar to what astronauts might encounter during a Mars mission developed more scar tissue around their hearts' small blood vessels compared with a control group. Although the findings haven't been validated in humans, the results contribute to growing concerns about the potential effects of long-haul space missions on astronauts' health.

Given these new findings, Live Science asked its readers whether they'd travel to the Red Planet, despite these potential health risks. Over 240 readers weighed in, with 65% picking "No, I don't think the risks are worth it."

Interestingly enough, the comments reflect a slightly different trend. One reader wrote, "Any pioneers who travel to Mars will be facing the prospect of death and never coming back. Even if they make it back, there are any number of health issues that could come up. These risks have not stopped people from exploring throughout human history. The bigger question is who to send?"

Other commenters echoed this sentiment, with another reader posting, "Explorers in the past have always been exposed to unusual risks; without the risk takers we would still believe the world is flat."

Beyond the feelings that Mars will be explored anyway, despite the health risks, other commenters highlighted the cost of these missions, with one reader stating, "Sending flesh water balloons to Mars is ridiculous. For the cost of one human on Mars, we could literally probe the entire solar system and map/sample/rove every planet and every major Moon."

Others highlighted the mental and emotional cost of a trip to Mars. "As a devoted sci-fi fan for decades, I always wished I could go into space," another reader noted. "Now that I am 77 years old, I realize I would miss the outdoors. The wind and trees, the birdsong and sweet grass under my feet would be a devastating loss. Let the brave explorers go instead. Life is too brief to leave this planet for the uncertainty and dangers of space travel."

Do you agree with these readers? Let us know your thoughts in the comments!

Kenna Hughes-Castleberry is the Content Manager at Live Science. Formerly, she was the Content Manager at Space.com and before that the Science Communicator at JILA, a physics research institute. Kenna is also a book author, with her upcoming book 'Octopus X' scheduled for release in spring of 2027. Her beats include physics, health, environmental science, technology, AI, animal intelligence, corvids, and cephalopods.

Apollo’s newest electric scooter is also its lowest-cost yet

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Apollo has spent the last several years carving out a spot for itself toward the more premium end of the electric scooter market. Now the company is trying something different: going after the mass market with its least expensive model yet.

The new Apollo Dash carries a $699 MSRP, but is launching at $599 as part of the company’s Black Friday sale running through November 30. More importantly, Apollo says the Dash isn’t intended to simply be a stripped-down budget scooter, but rather a way to bring much of its existing technology down to a lower price point.

The scooter uses a 48V electrical system powering a 600W nominal, 1,200W peak-rated Bosch motor, with a top speed of 20 mph (32 km/h). Its 480Wh battery is rated for up to 25 miles (40 km) of range in Eco mode.

Apollo also claims the 48V architecture avoids limiting motor power to anemic levels as the battery drains, meaning riders shouldn’t experience the same increasingly sluggish performance toward the bottom of the battery that can occur on some lower-cost scooters.

At 43 lb (19.5 kg), the Dash is reasonably portable, though getting the weight and price down comes with an important compromise: there’s no suspension. Apollo also caps rider weight at 220 lb (100 kg), and points heavier riders or those regularly tackling rough roads and hills toward the more expensive Apollo Go instead.

Despite the lower price, there’s still a surprisingly long feature list. The Dash gets IP66 water resistance, self-healing tubeless tires, regenerative braking, built-in turn signals, Apollo’s Beam stem light, NFC unlocking, Apple Find My integration, and connectivity with the Apollo smartphone app.

Apollo says it put the frame through 10,000 km (6,200 miles) of durability validation before shipping production units. A beta test program also accumulated 1,002 miles over 144 rides without any reported punctures or maintenance issues. Beta riders averaged 19 miles (30 km) per ride while finishing with 29% battery remaining, according to Apollo.

Perhaps the bigger story here, though, is where Apollo is positioning the Dash. The company says 52% of its customers previously owned scooters from brands including Segway, NIU, and Xiaomi, but historically moving up to an Apollo often meant crossing the $1,000 mark.

At $599, the Dash appears designed to get those riders into Apollo’s ecosystem much earlier – while also putting the company squarely into competition with the mass-market scooters it previously sat above.

And for those wanting a bit more power or features, Apollo’s more upmarket models are on sale now as part of early Black Friday promotions.

The e-bikes that make up Micah’s current daily drivers are the $999 Lectric XP 2.0, the $1,095 Ride1Up Roadster V2, the $1,199 Rad Power Bikes RadMission, and the $3,299 Priority Current. But it’s a pretty evolving list these days.

How to get the Shapeless Hunter Mask in The Division 2: Echoes of Central Park

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Shapeless Hunter Mask is one of the several secret items you can find in The Division 2 as part of the Echoes of Central Park DLC. You’ll come across cryptic clues at specific locations, and some of them will lead you to this mask.

This guide will tell you where you exactly need to look at to find these items.

For starters, you need to complete all the parts during night (between 9pm and 6am in-game time). First, we have to reach the safehouse near the Tower settlement. This location will be important as you’ll be traveling here a fair number of times.

Once you enter the house, you’ll find an entrance with “Help Us” written on top of it. Enter the room, follow the ringing telephone, and you’ll come to a room with a small TV, tape, and the following scribbling on the wall “Do you like scary games”.

Play the tape, and this will begin the activity. To find this mask, we will need to find several tapes, return to this room, and play them.

Once you reach the spot marked on the image above, you’ll find a courtyard with a yellow crane in the center. Use the scaffolding on top of the crane, and you’ll find a window opening attached to it.

Take the door on your left after entering the building, and you’ll reach the bathroom. You’ll find the tape in bathtub in the same bathroom, and this will be your first collectible.

Once you reach the spot marked on the image above, look for a playground with visible light on one side. Go to the source of the light, and you’ll find your second tape.

Go to the marked location, and you’ll find a building door with decorative lights. Go to the door, and you’ll find the tape to pick it up.

Once you reach the spot marked on the image above, you’ll find an area with flooded sections and a machine. Activate the machine to drain the water, and you’ll then find a half-buried skeleton with the tape on it.

The final tape can be found by going to the marked spot on the image above. Enter through the door opposite to the first one, and take the straight exit. You’ll come across another door that allows you to access the stairs.

Take the stairs that go below, and you’ll find the tape in the back of a room filled with pipes.

Once you’ll have claimed all five tapes, go to the marked location to reach the High Low Electronics. You’ll find a final tape to summon the hunter.

The song was produced by the Bolt Cutters — Apple, Davíd Garza, Sebastian Steinberg, and Amy Aileen Wood, with each of their parts recorded remotely from locales including Los Angeles, Houston, and from the East Coast. “Amy masterfully brought them together to capture this moment’s energy,” they said in a statement.

The powerful song is an apt choice to cover now following this week’s midterm elections results, a blue tsunami happily celebrated by many music fans. “There’s a new world a comin’ near/Singing loud and clear/Revolution,” Apple soulfully sings. “Feel that rumblin’ along the ground; It’s a mighty sound/Revolution.”

Especially for those deaths and disappearances of young people that remain unsolved and poorly explained.”

Honda Dealer Sued For 'Obnoxious' AI Robocalls To A Guy Who Didn't Want To Sell Them His Car

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

You get them. I get them. We all get them. AI robocalls are a nightmare scourge on society that are driving everybody to the brink of their sanity. Usually, they're from some sort of money-lending organization. Still, one Massachusetts man says he keeps receiving pre-recorded phone calls from Cambridge Honda asking him whether he was interested in selling them his car. Now, he's seeking class-action status against the dealership, alleging that it violated the Telephone Consumer Protection Act.

Plaintiff Jonathan Kamens has never been a customer at Cambridge Honda, and his cell phone number is on the National Do Not Call Registry, according to Automotive News. The complaint alleges that an AI-generated caller with a "robotic and artificial voice" addresses Kamens by the wrong name, "Timothy," and then says, "Roger from Cambridge Honda. Reaching out because you're one of our customers, and your car happens to be on a list of models we are looking to buy. If you're open to an instant cash offer on your car, give me a call at (351) 300-0683. Thanks." Kamens added that he received subsequent texts from "Roger" inviting him to sell his vehicle — something he really has no interest in doing.

In an effort to get answers, Kamens called the number back. A voice at the other end answered, but when he started the conversation asking if the person was real or not, it said it was AI, according to AutoNews, which received the same automated response when it called the number.

Kamens says he filed the suit on behalf of everyone in the U.S. who has received a pre-recorded message from Cambridge Honda on their cell phones over the past four years. The complaint asserts that folks who got these calls were "temporarily deprived of legitimate use of their phone because the phone line was tied up, they were charged for the calls and their privacy was improperly invaded."

"Moreover, the call injured [Kamens] because it was frustrating, obnoxious, annoying and a nuisance and disturbed [his] solitude," it said.

While this is true, it's also funny to think about suing someone because they took away from your screen time. I mean, I would, but you get what I'm saying.

The lawsuit, filed in the U.S. District Court for the District of Massachusetts, asked for certification of the class, damages and an order prohibiting Cambridge Honda from "making calls, except for emergency purposes, to any cellular telephone numbers using an artificial or prerecorded voice in the future." The Honda dealer, for its part, has remained quiet. A lawyer representing the store said she wasn't authorized to discuss the case.

It's not clear how many times Kamens was contacted by the dealer or how many people in total were impacted, but if they're going for a class action, my gut tells me it's not "a few."

Godzilla Minus Zero Finally Explains How Noriko Survived Minus One

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This article contains spoilers for "Godzilla Minus Zero."

One of the most anticipated sequels of the year is finally upon us. "Godzilla Minus Zero" is the follow-up to 2024's "Godzilla Minus One," with director Takashi Yamazaki returning to once again put his stamp on the legendary franchise. And since "Godzilla Minus One" was officially the first movie in the franchise to win an Oscar, the pressure is on.

Thus far, the first reactions to "Godzilla Minus Zero" have been glowing, so it's fair to say mission accomplished. A big reason for that is because Yamazaki picked up where he left off, centering the action on the same characters two years later as Japan attempts to dig itself out of the devastation left behind by Godzilla's attacks. Specifically, Ryûnosuke Kamiki is back as Kōichi Shikishima and Minami Hamabe is back as Noriko as they both try to build a life for their adopted daughter, Akiko (Sae Nagatani).

As viewers will surely recall, Noriko was left for dead after Godzilla annihilated the Ginza District in "Minus One," which is one of the worst things that Godzilla has ever done. Miraculously and somewhat inexplicably, Noriko survived the attack. Shikishima and Akiko reunited with her in the hospital at the very end of the movie, and all seemed well — or mostly well, anyway. Noriko had a very strange, scaly growth on her neck, implying that something was amiss.

One of the biggest questions viewers were left with was how in the heck Norkiko survived Godzilla's devastating blast. It seemed like it was just for the sake of giving the movie a happy ending. However, "Minus Zero" not only explains how Noriko survived, but uses that as a major plot device that the sequel hinges on.

"Godzilla Minus Zero" shows the horrifying aftermath of a kaiju attack like never before, revisiting key events from "Minus One" from new perspectives. One of these scenes explains how Noriko survived. We meet Dr. Ogata (Jun Kunimura), who treated Noriko after the attacks in 1947. He explains that there's no way this woman should have survived. She was on death's door — so much so that he told a nurse to leave her and tend to someone else.

Miraculously, the following day, she was breathing with a strong heartbeat. Within days of that, her wounds had all but disappeared, save for her eye. The explanation? Godzilla's G cells were infused into several survivors. These cells have regenerative properties, which explains why Noriko's terrible wounds healed. Unfortunately, it's not all good news.

Every other person who was infected with the G cells died, with scaly growths emerging from their skin. This explains the markings on Noriko's neck at the end of "Minus One." But she was also the only one who managed to live this long with the G cells in her body. Those G cells are later used to help lure Godzilla to the final battle with King Ghidorah, using Dr. Murakami's amplification device. Throughout the movie, Noriko says she doesn't have much time left, as the scaly growths continue to spread.

In retrospect, it's interesting that the happy ending in "Godzilla Minus One" almost didn't happen. That ending teed up much of the sequel's plot and some of the more harrowing scenes in the franchise. Whether or not Noriko survives much longer is an open-ended question, but at the very least, Takashi Yamazaki didn't merely save her for the sake of putting a nice bow on the previous movie. There was a meaningful, in-universe explanation for that decision.
